# TANTRIK TEXTS
<note>Tantrik refers to the ancient Indian traditions of ritual, meditation, and philosophy known as Tantra.</note>
->EDITED BY ARTHUR AVALON<-
<note>Arthur Avalon was the pseudonym used by Sir John Woodroffe, a British Orientalist who played a key role in introducing Tantric philosophy to the West.</note>
->VOL. IX<-

<image-desc>A detailed line drawing of a Shri Yantra, a complex sacred geometric diagram. It consists of nine interlocking triangles (five pointing downwards, four pointing upwards) centered around a central point <term>bindu; the point of origin and unity</term>. These triangles are surrounded by two concentric lotus-petal circles: an inner ring of eight petals and an outer ring of sixteen petals. The entire circular design is enclosed within a square outer frame featuring T-shaped steps or "gates" <term>bhupura; the "earth-city" or protective enclosure</term> on each of the four sides.</image-desc>

<meta>This page transitions from the general bibliography of Arthur Avalon’s works (such as "The Serpent Power" and "Wave of Bliss") to the specific preface of the current volume, a translation of a Tantric hymn dedicated to the goddess Kali.</meta>

# PREFACE

THIS celebrated Kaula Hymn <note>original: *Kaula Stotra*; the Kaula tradition is a major school of Tantra emphasizing the worship of the Divine Mother</note>, which is now translated from the Sanskrit for the first time, is attributed to Great Time <note>original: *Mahâkâla*; a fierce form of Shiva who presides over time and dissolution</note> Himself. The Text used is that of the edition published at Calcutta in 1899 by the Sanskrit Press Depository, with a commentary in Sanskrit by the late Great Teacher <note>original: *Mahâmahopâdhyâya*—a title for an eminent scholar</note> Krishnanâtha Nyâya-panchânana, who was both very learned in Tantric Scripture <note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note> and faithful to his Path <note>original: *Dharma*; his religious and ethical duty</note>. He thus refused the offer of a good Government Post made to him personally by a former Lieutenant-Governor on the ground that he would not accept money for imparting knowledge.

Some variants in reading are supplied by this commentator. I am indebted to him for the Notes, or substance of the notes, marked K. B. To these I have added others, both in English and Sanskrit explaining matters and allusions familiar doubtless to those for whom the original was designed, but not so to the English or even ordinary Indian reader. I have also referred to the edition of the *Hymn* <note>original: *Stotra*</note> published by Ganesha Chandra Ghosha at Calcutta in 1891, with a translation in Bengali by Gurunâtha Vidyânidhi, and commentary by Durgârâma Siddhântavâgîsha Bhattâchâryya. I publish for the first time Vimalânanda Svâmî's Commentary to which I again refer later. When in this Introduction or in the Commentary I have not mentioned these two works my authorities are the Tantras or Tântrik works which I cite, or the information I have gathered from those whom I have consulted.

One of the chief features of this *Hymn* is that it gives the extraction of the mantra <note>original: *mantroddhâra*; the process of revealing or constructing a secret sacred sound</note> of Kali of the South <note>original: *Dakshinâ Kâlikâ*; the most common and benevolent form of the goddess Kali</note>. It not only gives us the Meditation imagery <note>original: *Dhyâna*</note>, Sacred diagram <note>original: *Yantra*</note>, Spiritual practice <note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note> and Description of the Essential Form <note>original: *Svarûpa-varnanâ*</note> of the Great Goddess <note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note>, but it also contains the chief Mantras of Kali of the South. The adjective "arising from the extraction of Your mantra" <note>original: *Tava manu-samuddharanajanu*</note> qualifying "this hymn" <note>original: *idam stotram*</note> in Verse <note>original: *Shloka*</note> 21 expressly states this fact.

Among the various Mantras of Kali of the South the greatest is the "Queen of Knowledge" <note>original: *Vidyâ-râjnî*</note> consisting of twenty-two syllables <note>original: *Dvâvimshâksharî*</note>.

<summary>This preface introduces a translated Sanskrit hymn dedicated to the goddess Dakshina Kalika, credited to the deity Mahakala and annotated by the scholar Krishnanatha Nyaya-panchanana. It highlights the text's importance in revealing the secret twenty-two-syllable mantra and the specific ritual practices associated with the goddess.</summary>

<keywords>Kaula Stotra, Mahakala, Dakshina Kalika, Krishnanatha Nyaya-panchanana, Tantra, mantra extraction, Kali worship, Sanskrit translation, Vidya-rajni, Arthur Avalon</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of previous translations and commentaries, focusing now on the structure of the specific hymn's primary mantra as decoded from the opening verses.</meta>

# PREFACE

This mantra gives the fullest and the truest symbol of Her Essential Form <note>original: *Svarûpa*; the true nature or ontological essence of a deity</note>. This mantra is contained in the first five verses <note>original: *Shlokas*</note>.

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| The first Verse contains | *Krîm, Krîm, Krîm* | (3 syllables <note>original: *aksharas*</note>) |
| 2nd ” ” ” | *Hûm, Hûm* | (2 ” ) |
| 3rd ” ” ” | *Hrîm, Hrîm* | (2 ” ) |
| 4th ” ” ” | *Dakshine Kâlike* <note>meaning: “O South-facing Kali”</note> | (6 ” ) |
| 5th ” ” ” | *Krîm, Krîm, Krîm, Hûm, Hûm,* | |
| | *Hrîm, Hrîm, Svâhâ* | (9 syllables) |

So the first five verses give us altogether 22 syllables, that is, the full Queen of Knowledge <note>original: *Vidyârâjnî*; the supreme mantra of a goddess</note>.

In Vimalânanda Svâmî’s Commentary <note>original: *Tîkâ*</note> of the 5th verse in the revised Sanskrit text, he has proved by quotations from the 9th chapter <note>original: *patala*</note> of the *Flowing River of Shaktic Bliss* <note>original: *Shâktânanda-taranginî*</note> that this 22-syllabled mantra is the full and true representation of the Essential Form <note>original: *Svarûpa*</note> of the Great Goddess <note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note>. See the quotation which begins with:
> “The sound *Krîm* is the head, O Goddess, and the sound *Krîm* is the forehead” <note>original: “*Krîm-kâro mastakam devi Krîm-kârashcha lalâtakam*”</note>
and ends with:
> “With the word *Svâ* are the two feet, and with the sound *hâ* are the nails.” <note>original: “*Svâ-shabdena pada-dvandvam hâ-kârena nakham tathâ*”; this describes how the mantra syllables correspond to the physical body of the Goddess</note>

The words “The Essential Form” <note>original: *Svarûpam*</note> (5th verse) and “The Whole” <note>original: *Sakalam*</note> (6th verse) point to this Queen of Knowledge. After the full mantra has been given in the first five verses, the 6th verse gives the various other Mantras of less importance and significance—ranging from one-syllabled to nine-syllabled, 15-syllabled, 21-syllabled and so forth.

This Extraction of the Mantra <note>original: *Mantroddhâra*; the process of revealing a hidden mantra from a poetic text</note> has been made following the authority of the *Revelation of Kali* <note>original: *Kâlikâ-shruti*</note>, the *Niruttara Tantra* and other Sacred Texts <note>original: *Tantras*</note>. Many commentators, however, have apparently in the view of Vimalânanda failed to consult the above authorities, and have thus fallen into errors and have given a different Revelation of the Mantra. Some take the 1st verse to give a one-syllabled mantra, the 2nd verse as also the 3rd to be two two-syllabled mantras, the 5th a nine-syllabled one and so on: a view which it is contended is opposite to such passages as “Now, this one in the aperture of the crown, attaining the form of the Absolute... one should utter the wife of the Great Sun <note>original: *vrihad-bhânu-jâyâm*; a poetic reference to the word *Svâhâ*, the wife of the Fire-god Agni</note>” <note>original: “*atha hainam brahma-randhre brahma-svarûpinîm âpnoti...........vrihad-bhânu-jâyâm uchcharet*”</note> in the 1st Hymn <note>original: *Sûkta*</note> of the *Kâlikopanishad*; or passages in the *Niruttara-Tantra* (Chapter II) beginning with “Now I shall speak...” <note>original: “*Atha vakshye*...”</note>

<summary>This page explains the construction of a 22-syllabled mantra dedicated to the goddess Kali, decoded from the first five verses of the hymn. It argues, using various Tantric authorities, that these syllables represent the actual physical and spiritual form of the Great Goddess.</summary>
<keywords>Kali mantra, 22-syllabled mantra, Vidyarajni, Svarupa, Mantroddhara, Vimalananda Svami, Tantra, Shlokas, Krim, Svaha</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the 22-syllabled mantra of Kali. It provides textual evidence from traditional scriptures and commentaries to justify why the first five verses of the hymn are structured to reveal this specific sacred sound-formula.</meta>

# PREFACE

“O Queen of the Kula lineage, the Mantra of South-facing Kali” <note>original: *Kuleshâni Dakshinâ-kâlikâ-manum*</note> and ending with “The Knowledge consisting of all mantras, which causes the creation, maintenance, and dissolution of the universe” <note>original: *Sarva-mantra-mayî vidyâ srishti-sthityanta-kârinî*</note>. The Master <note>original: *Svâmî*</note> further refers me to the end of the *Upanishad of the Goddess Kali* <note>original: *Kâlikopanishad*</note> where, dealing with the various sacred formulas <note>original: *Mantras*</note> of the South-facing Kali <note>original: *Dakshinâ Kâlikâ*</note>, it is said: “Now, one should chant the entire Knowledge <note>The ‘Knowledge’ or *Vidyâ* here refers specifically to the goddess’s mantra</note> by placing at the beginning one, two, or three seed-syllables, or the three-fold names” <note>original: *Atha sarvâm vidyâm prathamam ekam dvayam vâ trayam vâ nâma-trayaputitam vâ kritvâ japet*</note>. The great practitioner of the Tantras, Purnananda Giri, explaining the passage says: “The phrase ‘the entire Knowledge’ refers to the first seed-syllable or the first two seed-syllables of the previously mentioned twenty-two syllabled mantra, and so on” <note>original: *Sarvâm vidyâm-iti pûrvokta-dvâvimshatyaksharyâh prathama vîjam vâ vîja-dvayam vâ*; see the *Mystery of the Dark Goddess* (*Shyâmâ-rahasyam*), Rasikamohan's edition, page 36</note>.

From the above consideration, it is clear that at the very beginning in the first five verses <note>original: *Shlokas*</note>, the 22-syllabled mantra is given, followed by the others. It may be added here that the fact of the Great Lord of Time <note>original: *Mahâkâla*; an epithet of Shiva</note> composing the Hymn in exactly 22 verses—no more, nor less—is also an indication of the correctness of the Master’s view. In further support of it, he cites five verses dealing with the extraction of the mantra <note>original: *Mantroddhâra*; the process of decoding a mantra from poetic text</note> from the *Hymn of the Succession* <note>original: *Krama-stava*</note> of the South-facing Kali, placed under the first five verses of this *Hymn to Kali* <note>original: *Karpûrâdi*</note>, which will be found in the printed text.

In the course of revising his commentary <note>original: *Vyâkhyâ*</note>, Vimalananda Svami has, in the first six verses, given good grounds to prove that the Hymn <note>original: *Stotra*</note> not only contains the decoding of the mantra and the spiritual practice <note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note> of the Blessed South-facing Kali, but also contains the mantras and secret worship <note>original: *Rahasyapûjâ*</note> of the Blessed Goddesses Tara and Tripura-sundari <note>Two other important forms of the Divine Mother in the ten Great Wisdom Goddesses</note>.

In addition to the decoding of the mantra, the following matters are contained in the Hymn:

| Subject | Verse Numbers |
|---|---|
| Meditation and Visualization <note>original: *Dhyâna*</note> | 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 11. |
| Sacred Geometric Diagram <note>original: *Yantra*</note> | 18. |
| Spiritual Practice <note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note> | 10, 11, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 20. |
| Ritual Use of Wine <note>original: *Madya*</note> | 13. |
| Ritual Use of Meat <note>original: *Mâmsa*</note> | 19. |
| Ritual Union <note>original: *Maithuna*</note> | 10. |
| Merits of Reciting the Hymn <note>original: *Phala-shruti*</note> | 21, 22. |

Verses 9, 12, and 14 contain only praise <note>original: *stuti*</note>.

Verses 10, 15 through 18, and 20 refer to the Tantric spiritual practice of the Heroic Path <note>original: *vîrâchâra-sâdhanâ*</note>. This Heroic Path is intended for the class of practitioner <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note> who are...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual requirements for the 22-syllabled mantra of the goddess Kali, specifically distinguishing between the different levels of spiritual practitioners and the timing of their worship.</meta>

# PREFACE

the "Heroic disposition" <note>original: *vîrabhâva*; a state of advanced spiritual practice where the seeker confronts and overcomes worldly desires</note> and the "Empowered" <note>original: *abhishikta*; one who has undergone formal ritual initiation</note>. To those who follow the "Way of the Animal" <note>original: *pashvâchâra*; the preliminary stage of worship for those still bound by worldly instincts and social conventions</note> this ritual is strictly forbidden. The nature of the "Secret Worship" <note>original: *rahasyapûjâ*</note> is indicated in the text, to which I have added an explanatory commentary in English and Sanskrit.

To the "Animal" practitioner <note>original: *Pashu*; one who is "bound" by the eight-fold noose of shame, doubt, fear, etc.</note>, spiritual practice <note>original: *sâdhana*</note> by night is prohibited, for it connotes in the "Goddess-oriented spiritual path" <note>original: *Shâkta-sâdhanâ*</note>, worship with the "Five Elements" <note>original: *Panchatattva*; often involving substances like wine and meat which are considered dangerous for the uninitiated</note>. The *Pashu* is still bound by the "noose" <note>original: *pâsha*</note> of desire and other worldly ties, and he is, therefore, not a "qualified candidate" <note>original: *adhikârî*</note>, for that which, if undertaken by the unfit, will only make these bonds stronger. For him, on the contrary, there are severe restrictions in this matter, for, as the *Manual of the Goddess Path* <note>original: *Shâktakrama*</note> cited by the commentator says, “He should avoid sexual union, conversation on the subject, and the company of those engaged in it” <note>original: *Maithunam tatkathâlâpang tadgoshthîng parivarjjayet*</note>. The *Pashu* should avoid the eight forms of sexual activity known as "eight-fold union" <note>original: *ashtânga maithuna*</note>—namely, thinking upon it <note>original: *smaranam*</note>, talking of it <note>original: *kîrtanam*</note>, play with women <note>original: *kelih*</note>, looking upon women with desire <note>original: *prekshanam*</note>, talking in private with women <note>original: *guhyabhâshanam*</note>, the wish or resolve for union <note>original: *sangkalpah*</note>, determination towards it <note>original: *adhyavasâyah*</note>, as well as the actual physical accomplishment <note>original: *kriyânishpattih*</note>. The *Nityâ Tantra*, which the commentator cites, says: “He (the *Pashu*) should never worship the Goddess during the latter part of the day or in the evening or at night” <note>original: *Râtrau naiva yajed devîng sandhyâyâng vâ’parâhnake*</note>. To this, from amongst many other authorities, I may add the *Svatantra*, which says that the practitioner in the "Animal" state <note>original: *Pashubhâva Sâdhaka*</note> should do one hundred thousand recitations of the mantra <note>original: *japa*</note> in the day time and that a "Heroic practitioner" <note>original: *Vîra*</note> devoted to his own "Path" <note>original: *Âchâra*</note> should do one hundred thousand recitations at night;

->“A practitioner devoted to the animal state should perform his recitations in the daytime.”<-
-><note>original: *Pashubhâvarato mantrî divâ laksha-japang charet*</note><-
->“The Heroic seeker devoted to his own path should perform his recitations at night.”<-
-><note>original: *Svâchâranirato vîro râtrau laksha-japang charet*</note><-

In connection with this verse I must observe that in the notes to verse 20 it is said that the first half of the 20th verse <note>original: *Shloka*</note> is meant for "Animal practitioners" <note>original: *Pashusâdhakas*</note> and that the second half refers to the "fully initiated heroic practitioner" <note>original: *pûrnâbhishiktavîrasâdhaka*</note>, as also that the word “afterwards” <note>original: *param*</note> means and refers to the time when the "Animal" practitioner, having received the "empowerment ritual" <note>original: *abhisheka*</note>, enters the "Heroic path" <note>original: *vîrâchâra*</note> and is...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual requirements for the 22-syllabled mantra of the goddess Kali, specifically regarding whether certain nocturnal rites are permitted for the "Animal-level" practitioner or are reserved for more advanced seekers.</meta>

# PREFACE

qualified candidate <note>original: *adhikârî*</note> for the midnight ritual repetition <note>original: *purashcharana*; a preliminary rite involving the repeated chanting of a mantra to "awaken" its power</note>. *Vimalânanda* <note>the commentator and teacher providing these insights</note> tells me that this is wrong and that the whole verse <note>original: *Shloka*</note> has reference to the "heroic" <note>original: *vîra*</note> or "divine practitioner" <note>original: *divya sâdhaka*; the highest level of spiritual seeker in the Tantric tradition</note> and that no portion of it refers to the "Animal practitioner" <note>original: *Pashu-sâdhaka*; a seeker who is still bound by social conventions and lacks the spiritual maturity for advanced rites</note>.

The quotation just made from the *Independent Tantra* <note>original: *Svatantra Tantra*</note> no doubt seems to lend support to the view that the first part of the verse refers to the "Animal" seeker, but he informs me—and I fully accept the correction—that he and other followers of the scripture <note>original: *Shâstra*</note> knew the passage to bear a meaning which is consonant with his view, that is, it means this:—The "Practitioner" <note>original: *Mantrî*; literally "one who possesses the mantra"</note> means the heroic seeker; the practitioner should perform the hundred-thousand-fold repetition <note>original: *laksha-japa*</note> in the day time following the conduct <note>original: *âchâra*</note> of the animal seeker ("devoted to the animal disposition" <note>original: *pashu-bhâva-ratah*</note>). The heroic seeker should perform the hundred-thousand-fold repetition in the night following his own conduct ("devoted to his own conduct" <note>original: *svâchâra-niratah*</note>). The word “own conduct” points to his interpretation being correct.

In support of his view the *Master* <note>original: *Svâmî*</note> cites the following Verses which all say the same thing namely that the initiate should be a "continent student" <note>original: *Brahmachârî*</note> during day and at night worship according to the "Way of the Kula" <note>original: *Kulâchâra*; the advanced, often secret, path of the Shakta tradition</note>. The *Necklace of the Kaula* <note>original: *Kaulâvalî*</note> says:

> Eating at night, consuming sacrificial food, he should repeat the mantra during the day while pure. 
> Clad in two garments, the hero should always be as a continent student. 
> At night he should worship the Goddess according to the sequence of the Kula-conduct. 
> For all twice-born men, a twofold rule is stated here.
> <note>original: *Naktam bhojî havishyânnam japed vidyâm divâ shuchih / Dvivâsâh sarvathâ vîro brahmachârî bhavet sadâ / Râtrau sampûjayed devîm kulâchâra-kramena tu / Dvijanmanâm tu sarveshâm dvidhâ vidhir ihochyate.*</note>

Again, the *Kalika Upanishad* <note>original: *Kâlikopanished*</note> says:

> Devoted to the Shambhavi-initiations <note>original: *Shâmbhava-dîkshâ*; a high form of spiritual initiation where the teacher transmits power through a glance, touch, or thought</note> or to the Shakti-path, during the day one should be a continent student; 
> at night, naked and with a mind always intent on sexual union, one should perform the rules of mantra-repetition and worship.
> <note>original: *Shâmbhava-dîkshâsu ratah shâkteshu vâ divâ brahmachârî râtrau nagnah sadâ maithunâsaktamânasah japa-pûjâdi-niyamam kuryâd iti.*</note>

The *Necklace of the Kaula* again says:

> Now the specific rule for the Goddess Kalika is stated. 
> During the day, with the discipline of a continent student, one should perform one's own number of repetitions. 
> At night, with meat, wine, fish, parched grain, and the products of sexual union.
> <note>original: *Unmukhyâh Kâlikâyâsh cha visheshah kathyate 'dhunâ / Divase brahmacharyena svîyasamkhyâjapang charet / Râtrau mâmsâsavairmatsyairmudrâbhir maithunodbhavaih.* These are the "Five M's" or *Panchatattva* of Tantric ritual.</note>

The reason for the heroic seeker <note>original: *vîrasâdhaka*</note> being instructed to adopt the conduct of a continent student in the day-time is the necessity for the concealment of the "heroic conduct" <note>original: *vîrâchâra*</note> from the public which Tantra so

<summary>This page clarifies that advanced Tantric rituals involving the "Five M's" (wine, meat, etc.) are strictly reserved for heroic seekers at night, who must appear as conventional, celibate students during the day to keep their practices secret.</summary>
<keywords>Vimalananda, heroic practitioner, vira sâdhaka, animal practitioner, pashu, Tantric secrecy, Kalika Upanishad, Kaulavali, Panchatattva, ritual timing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Heroic" versus "Animal" spiritual dispositions, specifically addressing the secrecy of the advanced rites and the strict prohibitions against certain practices for those not yet spiritually qualified.</meta>

often insists upon. Shiva says that the **Heroic Path** <note>original: *vîrâchâra*; the "Hero's Way" of Tantric practice involving rituals that challenge social norms</note> cannot be understood aright by the common people and therefore must be concealed as closely as a man should conceal his own mother's secret lover <note>original: *gopayet mâtri-jâra-vat*; though the OCR reads "sin," the Sanskrit *jâra* refers to a paramour, emphasizing the absolute necessity of keeping these practices hidden from the uninitiated</note>.

Moreover, the worship of the Goddess **Kali** in the **"Animal Way"** <note>original: *pashvâchâra*; the path of the seeker who is still bound by worldly instincts and social conventions</note> is totally forbidden by Shiva. The "Animal" seeker <note>original: *Pashu*</note> is precluded by the **Tantra** <note>scriptures of the esoteric tradition</note> from the worship of Kali. For example, the *Niruttara Tantra* says:

> *Without the Divine disposition or the Heroic disposition, one should not worship Kali.*
> *By such worship, one goes to hell and suffers pain at every step.*
> *If a man devoted to the Animal disposition worships Kali,*
> *Then he goes to the Raurava Hell until the time of the final dissolution.*
> <note>original Sanskrit: *Divya-bhâvam vîra-bhâvam vinâ Kâlim prapûjayet / Pûjane narakam yâti tasya duhkham pade pade / Pashubhâva-rato devi yadi Kâlim prapûjayet / Rauravam narakam yâti yâvad âhûta-samplavam.*</note>

(By the worship of Kali without the **Divine disposition** <note>original: *Divyabhâva*</note> and **Heroic disposition** <note>original: *Vîrabhâva*</note> the worshipper suffers pain at every step and goes to hell. If a man who is of the **Animal disposition** <note>original: *Pashubhâva*</note> worships Kali then he goes to the **Raurava Hell** <note>a specific level of hell in Hindu cosmology characterized by intense suffering</note> until the time of final dissolution).

**Vimalânanda Svâmi** <note>the commentator</note> says: The worship of Kali without the use of wine, though seen in many places, is **derived from the Puranas** <note>original: *Paurânik*; referring to more mainstream, orthodox Hindu scriptures</note> and not **Tantric** <note>original: *Tântrik*; i.e., not sanctioned by the esoteric Tantra scriptures</note>.

Verses 1-8, 11, the first part of verse 20, and 21 (except at midnight) deal with the **ritual repetition** <note>original: *japa*</note> of the **sacred sound** <note>original: *mantra*</note> of, and **meditative visualization** <note>original: *dhyâna*</note> upon, the **Goddess** <note>original: *Devî*</note>, which, of course, may be done by the "Animal" seeker. Verses 9, 12, 13, and 14 are **hymns of praise** <note>original: *stuti*</note>; and verse 22 is the usual **verse regarding rewards** <note>original: *phalashloka*</note>, which states the reward to be gained by the reading of the **Hymn** <note>original: *Stotra*</note>.

Verses 10, 15-18, and the second portion of verse 20 deal with **"Creeper-practice"** <note>original: *Latâsâdhana*; a ritual involving a female partner</note>. The **power** <note>original: *shakti*</note> of this **spiritual practice** <note>original: *sâdhana*</note> is ordinarily the own wife of the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note>, married according to the **Vedic** <note>original: *Vaidik*; the ancient, orthodox rituals of India</note> injunctions; she is known as "one's own power" or the **"Primal Power"** <note>original: *svashakti* or *âdyâshaktî*</note>, as she is technically called in Tantra. One's own wife is the Primal Power, and spiritual practice should be done with her aid (*"One's own wife should be the Primal Power; relying on her, one should perform the practice"*). With her is practiced that **ritual power-practice** <note>original: *shakti-sâdhana*</note>, the aim of which is the acquirement of self-control, which, checking the outward-going current, places the seeker upon the **path of return** <note>original: *nivrittî*; the spiritual path of withdrawing from worldly desires</note>. Indeed, the *Kaulikârchanadîpikâ* <note>a Tantric ritual manual</note> says, "Without the **Primal Power** <note>original: *âdyâ shakti*</note> worship is but evil magic" (*"Worship without the Primal Power is intended for black magic"*). It is only the **perfected one** <note>original: *siddha*</note>, which term is here used in the special sense of one

<summary>This page discusses the strict requirements for worshipping the goddess Kali, warning that those with an "Animal" disposition risk spiritual ruin if they perform certain rites. It also introduces the concept of Latasadhana, explaining that a practitioner's wife serves as the essential spiritual partner or "Primal Power" in these advanced practices.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the qualifications for advanced Tantric practice, moving from the prohibitions for the "Animal" seeker to the specific requirements for the "Heroic" seeker who has mastered his passions.</meta>

# PREFACE 7

<margin>?</margin>
...who has obtained complete control over his passions, to whom is permitted another **power** <note>original: *shakti*; here referring to a female ritual partner or the divine energy she embodies</note> (**Supreme Power** <note>original: *parashakti*</note>). So the *Prâna-toshinî* <note>a famous 19th-century Bengali Tantric compilation</note> quotes: "a man shall obtain **spiritual perfection** <note>original: *siddhi*</note> with his own **power** <note>his wife</note>, and afterwards (that is, when he is **perfected** <note>original: *siddha*</note>) he should perform **mantra recitation** <note>original: *japa*</note> with the **Supreme Power/another woman**" (*Svashaktau siddhim âpnuyât parashaktau tadâ japet*). And similarly the *Niruttara Tantra* says that the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note> who is perfected in **the Way of the Kulas** <note>original: *Kulâchâra*; an advanced lineage of Tantric practice</note> may worship "another" woman (*Siddhamantrî kulâchâre parayoshâm prapûjayet*). In both these cases, **Supreme Power** <note>original: *parashakti*</note> has a double meaning: namely, "another" woman (that is, a physical woman) or the "Supreme" woman, who within the body is the **Serpent Power** <note>original: *Kundalinî Shakti*; the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note>. This latter appears to be the sense in the quotation which speaks of the perfected practitioner. It has been said also, as in the *Mahânirvâna Tantra*, that this "other power" must (if unmarried) be married either by **Vedic** <note>traditional orthodox Hindu rites</note> or **Shaiva** <note>Tantric rites dedicated to Shiva</note> rites, or (if married and the husband is dead) according to the latter rite. Further, that which determines the moral character of an act is the intention with which it is done. As the *Kaulâvalîya* says, when a man's intention is bad then his act is so, otherwise there is no fault:

->*Therefore, when one's intention is vile,*<-
->*Then it results in fault; otherwise, there is no blame anywhere.*<-
<note>original: *Ata eva yadâ yasya vâsanâ kutsitâ bhavet / Tadâ doshâya bhavati nânyathâ dûshanang kvachit.*</note>

As an example of the same act and varying intention, it is aptly said: "A wife is kissed with one feeling and a daughter's face with another" (*Bhâvena chumbitâ kântâ bhâvena duhitrânanam*). A practitioner who is given over to lust—for the subjugation of which the **spiritual discipline** <note>original: *sâdhana*</note> is prescribed—goes, as is said in the *Tantrasâra*, to the Hell called **Raurava** <note>a specific "terrible" hell in Hindu cosmology</note> (*Lingayonirato mantrî rauravang narakang brajet*). In the words of the *Âchârabheda Tantra*: "One may be a **practitioner of the Left-hand Path** <note>original: *Vâmâchârî*</note> if one can worship the **Supreme Goddess** being oneself a woman" (*Vâmâchâro bhavet tatra vâmâ bhûtvâ yajet parâm*). This is on the principle that a worshipper should always be like the object of his worship. Woman is a **Deity** <note>original: *Devatâ*</note> and the embodiment of the **Supreme Power**, and is as such honored and worshipped; she is, when she is the **power to be worshipped** <note>original: *pûjyâ shakti*</note>, never the subject of mere physical enjoyment.

<summary>This page explains the role of the ritual partner (Shakti) in advanced Tantric practice, emphasizing that such rites are only for those with absolute self-control. It argues that the morality of these acts depends entirely on the practitioner's pure spiritual intention rather than lust.</summary>
<keywords>Shakti, Parashakti, Vamachara, Kulachara, spiritual intention, Kundalini, Tantric ethics, ritual marriage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of specific verses from the previous page, further defining the spiritual discipline required to transition from a mere practitioner to a perfected being.</meta>

# PREFACE

Verses 15 and 16, as sufficiently appears from their context, refer to the **spiritual practice** <note>original: *sâdhana*; the disciplined path of ritual or meditation</note> of those who are not yet **perfected** <note>original: *siddha*; one who has attained spiritual success</note>.

Verses 10, 17, and 18 apply to both the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note> and the **perfected** <note>original: *siddha*</note>; as to verse 20, see pp. 4, 5 earlier.

By such **spiritual practice** <note>original: *sâdhana*</note> the last vestiges of the most powerful of such bonds is sought to be destroyed, and with such destruction the seed of **action and consequence** <note>original: *karma*</note> and rebirth. He, like Shiva, becomes destroyer of **Desire** <note>original: *Smara*; a name for the God of Love, Kamadeva, whom Shiva burned to ashes</note>, and Shiva Himself. Verses 4, 18, and 20 refer directly to this fruit of **practice** <note>original: *sâdhanâ*</note>. Others indicate the material and intellectual greatness on earth of the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note>, who devoutly worships the **Goddess** <note>original: *Devî*</note>. To him is given mastery over all persons and things of the world, which on death, if **perfected** <note>original: *siddha*</note>, he leaves for the dwelling by the Supreme Feet (verse 17), or **liberation** <note>original: *Nirvâna*</note>. As Shiva says in the *Kâlivîlâsa Tantra*: "I have told you, my beloved, all about the five **Principles** <note>original: *Tattvas*; here referring to the ritual substances known as the 'Five Ms'</note>, **spiritual practice** <note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note> in the cremation ground and with the funeral pyre—now listen to the doctrine of the **Perfected Hero** <note>original: *Siddha-vîra*</note>."

->**Wine, fish, and meat, parched grain, and sexual union**<-
->**furthermore;**<-
->**Practice in the cremation ground, O auspicious one, and on the funeral pyre;**<-
->**All this I have told you; listen now to the doctrine of the perfected hero.**<-
<note>original Sanskrit: *Madyang matsyang tathâ mângsang mudrâng maithunam eva cha / Shmashânasâdhanam bhadre chitâsâdhanam eva cha / Etat te kathitang sarvang siddhavîramatang shrinu*</note>

It is the **practice** <note>original: *sâdhana*</note> of the cremation-ground on which all passion is burnt away. There are two kinds of cremation-ground, of which the one is the funeral pyre (**the pile** <note>original: *chitâ*</note>), and the other is **the Great Mother Kali in the form of the womb** <note>original: *yonirûpâ mahâkâlî*</note>. As the first Chapter of the *Niruttara Tantra* says, there are two cremation grounds, namely that which is the funeral pyre and the **womb** <note>original: *yoni*</note> which, in its **subtle** <note>original: *sûkshma*</note> sense, is the **Goddess** <note>original: *Devî*</note>, the **cremation ground** <note>original: *shmashâna*</note> being in the same sense dissolution or **the end of the world cycle** <note>original: *pralaya*</note>. (**The cremation ground is of two kinds, O Goddess: the funeral pyre and the womb, as it is proclaimed.** <note>original: *Shmashânang dvividhang devi chitâ yoni prakîrtitâ*</note>) In even the **gross/material** <note>original: *sthûla*</note> sense the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note> must be a **good practitioner** <note>original: *susâdhaka*</note>, for union without right disposition—**mantra recitation** <note>original: *japa*</note>, **meditation** <note>original: *dhyâna*</note> etc.—is merely the animal **sexual union** <note>original: *maithuna*</note> of an **animalistic man** <note>original: *pashu*; the lowest level of spiritual seeker who is still bound by social shame and physical lust</note>.

**Verse** <note>original: *Shloka*</note> 19 refers to animal and human sacrifice to Kâlî. Reference to this sacrifice is also made in the *Kâlikâ Purâna*, and the *Tantrasâra* speaks of a substitute in the figure of a man made of the paste of cereals. The latter work also

<summary>This page explains the transition from basic spiritual practice to the advanced "heroic" path, detailing the symbolic and literal meanings of the cremation ground as a site for destroying worldly passion. It emphasizes that ritual union must be accompanied by meditation to distinguish it from animalistic behavior.</summary>

# PREFACE

...says that by the sacrifice of a man one acquires great prosperity, and the **eight supernatural powers** <note>original: *ashtasiddhis*; these are miraculous abilities such as becoming as small as an atom or as heavy as the earth</note>. <note>original: *Naradatte maharddhih syâd ashtasiddhir anuttamâ*</note>. But it adds that this is not for all. For the **priest** <note>original: *Brâhmana*</note> may not make such a sacrifice. <note>original: *Brâhmanânâng naravalidâne nâdhikârah*; literally, "the Brahmans have no authority for the offering of a human sacrifice"</note>. And if he does so, he goes to Hell. Moreover according to K. B. <note>likely referring to the commentator Krishnananda Agamavagisha</note>, who cites as his authority the *Yâmala* <note>a class of Tantric texts</note> quoted in the *Kâlikalpalatâ*, the King alone can make such a sacrifice.

This leads one to point out that the Hymn has other than these **gross** <note>original: *sthûla*; refers to the literal or physical level of understanding</note> meanings. In the Brahmanic tradition, everything has three aspects—**Supreme** <note>original: *Para*; the highest spiritual truth</note>, **Subtle** <note>original: *Sûkshma*; the psychological or energetic level</note>, and **Gross** <note>original: *Sthûla*; the physical or literal level</note>. Thus the nineteenth **verse** <note>original: *shloka*</note> when referring to the sacrifice of various animals and of man himself intends, according to the subtle sense, the six great sins for which they stand, ranging from Lust (symbolized by the goat) to Pride (symbolized by man). It is these inner vices which must be sacrificed by the knowers who are worshippers of the Mother, the age of material sacrifice, so universal throughout the world, having passed away. So again the word **Supreme Power** <note>original: *Parashakti*</note> may refer to the Supreme Goddess or may be used in the sense of a female ritual partner other than the **practitioner's wife** <note>original: *svashakti*</note> who may, in the case of the **qualified practitioner** <note>original: *adhikârî*; one who has reached the necessary level of spiritual maturity</note>, be an associate in the worship on the principle stated in the *Guhyakâlîkhanda* <note>The Section on the Secret Kali</note> of the *Mahâkâla Samhitâ*.

> “As is the competency of the **practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhaka*</note>, so must be that of the **female practitioner** <note>original: *sâdhikâ*</note>. In this way only is success attained and not otherwise even in ten million years.”

->As is the practitioner described, so also must be the female practitioner;<-
->Thus one attains success, not otherwise even in ten million years.<-
<note>original: *Yâdrishah sâdhakah proktah Sâdhikâ’pi cha tâdrishî / Tatah siddhim avâpnoti nânyathâ varsha-kotibhih.*</note>

This principle rests on the fact that man and woman together make one whole and can only co-operate in the rites where the attainments or **spiritual qualification** <note>original: *adhikâra*</note> of each is the same. But this does not necessarily mean that such co-operation is by **ritual union** <note>original: *maithuna*</note> in its sexual sense; quite the contrary. In the same way in the **Vedic** <note>original: *Vaidik*; relating to the Vedas, the most ancient Hindu scriptures</note> ritual the wife is a **partner in religious duty** <note>original: *sahadharminî*</note>. But such ritual is only for the competent within the bounds of **scriptural** <note>original: *shâstric*</note> injunction for, as the *Shaktisangama Tantra* (Part...

<summary>The author explains that the ritual sacrifices mentioned in the text should be understood symbolically as the sacrifice of internal vices like lust and pride. He also emphasizes that spiritual practices involving a female partner require both individuals to possess equal spiritual qualification and maturity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of symbolic versus literal interpretations in Tantric practice, specifically addressing how the concept of "union" (Maithuna) refers to the internalizing of ritual acts and the movement of energy within the body.</meta>

# PREFACE

IV) says,—“Though a man be a knower of the three times—past, present, and future—and though he be a controller of the three worlds, even then he should not transgress the rules of conduct for men in the world, even if only in his mind.”

->*Though one may be a knower of the three times and capable of attracting the three worlds to oneself,*<-
->*Even then, one should not, even in the mind, transgress the customs of the world.*<-
<note>original: *Yadyapyasti trikâlajnas trailokyâkarshanakshamah / Tathâ'pi laukikâchâram manasâ'pi na langhayet.*</note>

<margin><image-desc>A pencil-drawn brace or squiggle in the margin.</image-desc></margin>
But **Supreme Power** <note>original: *Parashakti*</note> again may mean no woman at all, but Supreme **Power** <note>original: *Shakti*</note> or the Mother Herself whose forms they are; and in such sense the union of the **Practitioner** <note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note> is with the “woman” within himself—the **Coiled Power** <note>original: *Kundalinî Shakti*</note> who in **Union** <note>original: *Yoga*</note> unites with Her Supreme Husband, **Supreme Shiva** <note>original: *Paramashiva*</note>. (See Arthur Avalon’s *The Serpent Power*). The context must be known, as in the misunderstood saying “*By sexual union the great practitioner becomes equal to me, without doubt*,” <note>original: *Maithunena mahâyogî mama tulyo na sangshayah*</note> which does not mean, as a recent English work on Hinduism suggests, that by sexual connection <note>original: *Maithuna*</note> the **Great Practitioner** <note>original: *Mahâyogî*</note> becomes without doubt the equal of Shiva or God. This is on its face absurd, and had it not been that such criticism is clouded with prejudice, the absurdity would be recognized. How can sexual connection make anyone God or His equal? The person spoken of is a **Great Practitioner** <note>original: *Mahâyogî*</note> who, as such, has no connection physical or otherwise with women. **Union** <note>original: *Maithuna*</note> means “action and reaction” and “coupling,” and sexual intercourse is only *one form* of such coupling. Thus, when a **Sacred Sound** <note>original: *Mantra*</note> is recited, there is a coupling or **union** <note>original: *Maithuna*</note> of the lips. In **Union** <note>original: *Yoga*</note>, there is a coupling <note>original: *Maithuna*</note> of the active and changeless Principles of the Universe. The saying means that the **Great Practitioner** <note>original: *Mahâyogî*</note> who unites the **Coiled Power** <note>original: *Kundalî Shakti*</note> in his body with **Supreme Shiva** <note>original: *Paramashiva*</note> becomes himself Shiva.

So again it is said in an apparently alarming verse quoted by **Tarkâlangkâra** <note>a famous scholar and commentator</note> in his commentary on the **Great Liberation Tantra** <note>original: *Mahânirvâna*</note>.

->*One should cast the organ into the mother’s womb and press the sister’s breasts;*<-
->*By placing the foot upon the Teacher's head, one is never born again.*<-
<note>original: *Mâtri-yonau kshipet lingam bhaginyâh stanamardanam / Guror mûrdhni padang dattvâ punarjanma na vidyate.*</note>

<margin><image-desc>A pencil-drawn brace or squiggle in the margin.</image-desc></margin>
This verse in its literal sense means that if anyone commits incest with his mother and sister and places his foot on the head of his **Teacher** <note>original: *Guru*</note>, he is liberated and is never again...

<summary>This page explains that Tantric verses often use shocking "twilight language" (Sandhya Bhasha) to describe internal yogic processes, where terms like "sexual union" or "incest" actually refer to the energetic union of the Kundalini with the divine within the body.</summary>
<keywords>Maithuna, Kundalini, Parashakti, symbolism, yoga, Mahayogi, esoteric interpretation, Arthur Avalon, Tarkalangkara</keywords>

reborn. But of course that is not the meaning. The first half of the line refers to the placing of the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term> in the triangle situated in the <term>root energy center<note>original: *Mûlâdhâra*—the chakra located at the base of the spine</note></term> with the <term>self-originated symbol of Shiva<note>original: *Svayambhulinga*</note></term> in it, which triangle is called the <term>mother-source<note>original: *Mâtri-yoni*</note></term>. The <term>symbol<note>original: *Linga*</note></term> is the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term>. From this point upwards, after union with the <term>coiled power<note>original: *Kundalinî*</note></term>, the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term> is to be led. The union of the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term> with the <term>coiled power<note>original: *Kundalinî*</note></term> is spoken of in the second half of the first line. The <term>coiled power<note>original: *Kundalinî*</note></term> is the sister of the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term>, both being in the same body. The meaning of the last line is as follows:—after union of the <term>coiled power<note>original: *Kundalinî*</note></term> and the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term>, the united couple are led up to the <term>thousand-petalled lotus<note>original: *Sahasrâra*</note></term> in the head, which is situated above the twelve-petalled lotus, which again is the abode of the <term>spiritual teacher<note>original: *Guru*</note></term>. When the <term>practitioner of Yoga<note>original: *Yogî*</note></term> is above the twelve-petalled lotus, his feet may be described as being on the head of the <term>spiritual teacher<note>original: *Guru*</note></term>. Moreover, it is said that at this point the relationship of teacher and disciple ceases. The <term>mother-source<note>original: *Mâtri-yoni*</note></term> is also the term given to those sections of the fingers between the joints on which the counting of the <term>prayer recitation<note>original: *Japa*</note></term> or recital of the <term>sacred formula<note>original: *mantra*</note></term> is *not* to be done. If the <term>mother-source<note>original: *Mâtri-yoni*</note></term> suggests incest, then this verse is a prohibition of it—*“Leaving the mother-source, one should move through all other sources.”* <note>original: *Mâtri-yonim paritajya viharet sarva-yonishu*</note> There are many other technical terms in the <term>Tantric Scriptures<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term> which it is advisable to know before criticising it. One of the tests to which an intending disciple may be put consists in being questioned as to such passages. If he is a gross-minded or stupid man, his answer will show it.

In order therefore that the Hymn may be understood in its various aspects, I have given in the Notes explanations of or in respect of its <term>gross or literal meaning<note>original: *Sthûla*</note></term>. This is followed by the valuable commentary given to me some years ago and now first published, by **Vimalânanda Svâmî**, which is called *Exposition of the Essential Nature* <note>original: *Svarûpavyâkhyâ*</note>; that is, it gives the <term>subtle or inner sense<note>original: *Sûkshma*</note></term> or, as we should say in English, the inner sense or esoteric meaning according to the teaching of his own teacher, the Great Scholar **Râmânanda Svâmî Siddhântapanchânana**. The text books and Commentary are preceded by an admirable little essay of **Svâmî Vimalânanda** by way of Introduction to

<summary>The author clarifies that Tantric terms like "mother-source" (Matri-yoni) refer to spiritual anatomy or ritual finger positions rather than literal incest, explaining the ascent of the soul (Jivatma) to the crown chakra. # PREFACE

the *Bestower of Pure Bliss Exposition of Essential Nature* <note>original: *Vimalânandadâyinî svarûpa-vyâkhyâ*</note> on this "Lord of Hymns" which is commonly known as the *Hymn starting with Camphor* <note>original: *Karpûrâdi Stotra*</note> chanted by the Great Lord of Time <note>original: *Mahâkâla*—a fierce aspect of the deity Shiva</note> to, and in honor of, the Favorable Goddess Kali <note>original: *Dakshinâ Kâlikâ*</note>. It, as also the inner-sense Commentary, are written for those liberation-seeking <term>spiritual practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> who, worshipping the <term>Sacred Knowledge of the Goddess<note>original: *Shrîvidyâ*</note></term>, meditate not on the <term>gross physical form<note>original: *Sthûlamûrti*</note></term> but on the <term>essential reality of her true nature<note>original: *Svarûpa-tattva*</note></term> of the <term>Divine Wisdom in the form of Kali<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ Kâlikî*</note></term>. As such, many will be glad, as I was, to read it and will derive benefit therefrom.

I may note here that the <term>Master<note>original: *Svâmî*—referring to the commentator Swami Vimalananda</note></term> while revising the <term>Commentary<note>original: *Vyâkhyâ*</note></term>, has given a new interpretation of the line "*those who have eyes like the petals of the lotus used in the playful dance of the Goddess of Fortune, become the very forms of desire*" <note>original: *te Lakshmî-lâsya-lîlâ-kamala-dala-drishah kâma-rûpâh bhavanti*</note> in the 5th <term>verse<note>original: *Shloka*</note></term> and of "*she who is devoted to the great bliss of the essence of union*" <note>original: *rati-rasa-mahânanda-niratâm*</note> in the 13th <term>verse<note>original: *Shloka*</note></term>.

<margin>A hand-drawn curly bracket marks the following paragraph in the left margin.</margin>
On the attainment of <term>spiritual perfection<note>original: *siddhi*</note></term>, ritual ceases. There is neither sacrifice nor worship, nor <term>union-practice<note>original: *yoga*</note></term>, <term>preparatory rites<note>original: *purashcharana*</note></term>, <term>vows<note>original: *vrata*</note></term>, <term>mantra chanting<note>original: *japa*</note></term>, or other <term>ritual action<note>original: *karma*</note></term>. For all <term>spiritual practice<note>original: *sâdhana*</note></term> ceases when it has borne its fruit in <term>perfection<note>original: *siddhi*</note></term>. The <term>perfected adept of the path<note>original: *Siddha Kaula*</note></term> is beyond all rules.

For the meaning of these and other terms, the reader is referred to the Author's "Principles of Tantra" <note>original: *Tantratattva*</note>, "Shakti and Shâkta," "Serpent Power" and "Garland of Letters" which is a study on the <term>Science of Sacred Sound<note>original: *Mantra Shâstra*</note></term>; and for other Hymns to the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term>, his and Ellen Avalon's "Hymns to the Goddess," translated from the Sanskrit of the <term>Sacred Treatises<note>original: *Tantra*</note></term>, the <term>Ancient Lore<note>original: *Purâna*</note></term>, and the <term>Hymn to the Goddess<note>original: *Devîstotra*</note></term> of the philosopher <term>Shankara<note>original: *Shangkarâchâryya*</note></term>, which gives other specimens of the Hindu Hymnal, of which that now published is but one and a special type.

->Puri,<-
->30, May, 1922.<-

## INVOCATION

### PURPOSE

I make obeisance to the Lord <term>Master<note>original: *Guru*—the spiritual teacher who removes ignorance</note></term>, the wish-granting Tree of the <term>Celestial Beings<note>original: *Suras*</note></term>, eternal Consciousness and Bliss Itself, the highest of the highest, the <term>Absolute Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>, <term>the Auspicious One<note>original: *Shiva*</note></term> Himself. I make obeisance to Her who by Her <term>Divine Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> of the <term>three qualities of nature<note>original: *three Gunas*—Sattva (purity), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia)</note></term> creates, maintains, and at the end of the <term>Cosmic Age<note>original: *Kalpa*</note></term> withdraws the world, and then alone is. Devoutly I call to mind Her, the Mother of the whole universe, the <term>Favorable Goddess<note>original: *Shivâ*—the feminine form of Shiva</note></term> Herself.

---

### THE SACRED SYLLABLE

->Obeisance to the Supreme <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term>.<-

*Here follows an Introduction to the <term>"Bestower of Pure Bliss"<note>original: *Vimalânandadâyinî*</note></term> Commentary on that Lord of Hymns called the <term>"Hymn starting with Camphor"<note>original: *Karpûrâdi Stotra*</note></term> dedicated to <term>the Holy and Favorable Kali<note>original: *Shrîmat Dakshiṇâ Kâlikâ*</note></term>.*

The all-good and all-powerful <term>Supreme Lord<note>original: *Parameshvara*</note></term> is without beginning or end. Though in Himself <term>Beyond Qualities<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term>, He is the <term>Foundation<note>original: *Âdhâra*</note></term> of the three <term>qualities of nature<note>original: *Gunas*</note></term>. Though Himself formless, He creates, preserves, and withdraws the <term>manifest world<note>original: *Prapancha*</note></term> of extended matter by means of the <term>Veiling<note>original: *Âvaraṇa*</note></term> and <term>Projecting<note>original: *Vikṣhepa*</note></term> <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term> of His own <term>Cosmic Illusion<note>original: *Mâyâ*</note></term>, which can make that possible which seems impossible. The <term>Shvetashvatara Upanishad<note>An ancient sacred Sanskrit text of the Vedic tradition</note></term> says that by meditation was seen the <term>Self-Power<note>original: *Svashakti*</note></term> of the <term>Divine One<note>original: *Deva*</note></term>, who is the abode of all causes, associated with the <term>Principle of Time<note>original: *Kâlatattva*</note></term>. In the <term>Niruttara Tantra<note>A specific scripture within the Tantric tradition</note></term>, <term>the Auspicious One<note>original: *Shiva*</note></term> speaks of the three-eyed corpse-like One, <term>Attribute-less<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term> but also the seat of qualities associated with <term>Divine Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>. Though Himself without beginning, middle, or end, He creates and is the material Cause of the world which has a beginning, middle, and end. For this reason, the <term>Sacred Scriptures<note>original: *Tantras* and *Shâstras*</note></term> call Him the <term>Primal Lord<note>original: *Âdinâtha*</note></term> and the <term>Great Lord of Time<note>original: *Mahâkâla*</note></term>.

<summary>This page opens the text with a devotional invocation to the Guru and the Goddess, establishing the philosophical premise that the Supreme Reality is both formless and the active creator of the universe through Divine Power.</summary>
<keywords>Invocation, Guru, Brahman, Shiva, Shakti, Three Gunas, Maya, Creation, Tantra, Mahakala, Parameshvara</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological introduction from the previous page, expanding on the nature of the Supreme Lord as the personification of Time. It bridges the concept of the eternal, formless Absolute with the cyclical, measurable time of the physical universe, establishing the metaphysical foundation for the worship of the Goddess as the Power of Time.</meta>

# HYMN TO KÂLÎ

<term>Supreme Shiva<note>original: *Paramashiva*</note></term> and the <term>Supreme Absolute<note>original: *Paramabrahman*</note></term>. It is this unlimited, undivided, beginningless, and endless <term>Great Time<note>original: *Mahâkâla*—the personification of time that eventually dissolves all things</note></term> who is imagined to be limited by the Sun, Moon, and Planets, and, as such, is called by the names of <term>various measures of time<note>original: *Kalâ, Kâshthâ, Muhûrtta, Yâma, Paksha*—these refer to specific traditional units ranging from seconds to fortnights</note></term>, Day, Night, Month, Season, Half-year, Year, <term>Aeon<note>original: *Yuga*</note></term>, <term>Cosmic Cycle<note>original: *Kalpa*</note></term> and so forth. It is He who divides Time into these various measures, and as the <term>Individual Aspect<note>original: *Vyashti*—the particular or separated parts of a whole</note></term> is called by the names of those units. He is named <term>Supreme Shiva the Great Time<note>original: *Paramashiva Mahâkâla*</note></term> when creating, preserving and withdrawing the millions of worlds.

Apart from individual name and form, He exists as the <term>Universal Aggregate<note>original: *Samashti*—the totality of all individual parts</note></term> of them and the <term>Endless Supreme Greatness<note>original: *Paramo mahân*</note></term>. The <term>Scripture of Vishnu<note>original: *Vishnu Purâna*</note></term> says that the <term>Lord of Time<note>original: *Bhagavân Kâla*</note></term> is without beginning or end. From him appears the limited within creation. The <term>Veda of the Atharvans<note>original: *Atharvaveda*</note></term> says that Time created all beings <term>progeny<note>original: *Prajâ*</note></term>. He is the <term>Lord of Creatures<note>original: *Prajâpati*</note></term>. From Time was self-born the <term>Sage Kashyapa<note>original: *Kashyapa*—a foundational figure in Vedic cosmology</note></term> and <term>Spiritual Heat<note>original: *Tapas*—ascetic energy or discipline</note></term>. Great Time is all-knowing since He is all-pervading, dependent on none, and the <term>Soul<note>original: *Âtmâ*</note></term> of all. The <term>Scripture of the Tortoise<note>original: *Kûrma Purâna*</note></term> also says that he is the Supreme, imperishable, without beginning or end, all-pervading, independent, the Soul of all who <term>fascinates<note>original: *Manohara*—literally "stealing the mind"</note></term> all minds by His greatness. The <term>treatise of Kâlamâdhava<note>original: *Kâlamâdhava*</note></term> cites the <term>Vishnudharmottara<note>original: *Vishnudharmottara*</note></term> as saying that He is called <term>Time<note>original: *Kâla*</note></term> because of his <term>dissolving<note>original: *Kalanât*</note></term> of all beings, and He is the <term>Supreme Lord<note>original: *Parameshvara*</note></term> because He is Himself without beginning or end. Great Time is Himself <term>without attributes<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term> and <term>without action<note>original: *Nishkriya*</note></term>, but his <term>Divine Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> makes the Sun and other heavenly lights rise, stay and set.

It is by the Power of the <term>Divine Energy<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> of Time that men and other <term>living beings<note>original: *Jîvas*</note></term> are conceived in the womb, are born, attain childhood, boyhood, middle and old age and leave the world on death. In the <term>Book of Peace<note>original: *Shântiparva*</note></term> of the <term>Great Epic of India<note>original: *Mahâbhârata*</note></term>, the <term>Sage Vedavyâsa<note>original: *Vedavyâsa*</note></term> says that it is through Time that women bear, that birth and death occur, winter, summer and rains come, and the seed germinates. Even the gods of creation, maintenance, and destruction—<term>Brahmâ, Vishnu and Rudra<note>original: *Rudra*—a fierce form of Shiva</note></term>—appear, stay and disappear through the Power of Time. None can escape Its operation. The <term>Vishnu Samhitâ<note>original: *Vishnu Samhitâ*</note></term> says that even those <term>Gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term> who create and withdraw

<summary>This page defines Mahakala (Great Time) as the supreme, undivided reality that manifests as measurable time through its Divine Power (Shakti). It cites various scriptures to explain that all life, the cycles of nature, and even the high gods themselves are subject to the governance of Time.</summary>
<keywords>Mahakala, Time, Shakti, Creation, Dissolution, Brahman, Puranas, Mahabharata, Vyashti, Samashti</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of Time (Kâla) as the supreme force of dissolution. It moves from defining the masculine aspect of Time (Mahâkâla) to the feminine aspect (Mahâkâlî), establishing their fundamental unity. The text begins to recount the mythological origins of the Goddess's revelation to the gods and her compassionate appearance in the current dark age (Kaliyuga) to provide a path to liberation through the Tantras.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION<-

...the world are themselves withdrawn by <term>Time<note>original: *Kâla*</note></term>. Time is certainly then the stronger. <term>The Great Lord of Time<note>original: *Mahâkâla*</note></term> is called <term>The Great Power of Time<note>original: *Mahâkâlî*</note></term> because He is one and the same and not different from His eternal <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>. It is She who is the <term>Great Wisdom<note>original: *Mahâvidyâ*</note></term>, the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>, the <term>Great Illusionist<note>original: *Mahâmâyâ*—the power that creates the appearance of the material world</note></term>, and <term>She whose form is the Supreme Absolute<note>original: *Parabrahmarûpinî*</note></term>. As the <term>First Lord<note>original: *Âdinâtha*</note></term>, the Great Lord of Time is the first creator of the world, so the Power of the Great Lord of Time, the merciful Great Goddess of Time, is the <term>First Teacher<note>original: *Âdiguru*</note></term> of the world. The <term>Scripture of the Yoginis<note>original: *Yoginî Tantra*</note></term> says that the Great Goddess of Time is the Mother of the world, and one with the Great Lord of Time, as is shown in the <term>Image of the Lord who is Half-Woman<note>original: *Arddhanârîshvara Mûrtti*—a sacred depiction of Shiva and Shakti merged as one body, representing the union of masculine and feminine principles</note></term>.

It was this <term>Knowledge of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term> who (according to the 10th Chapter of the *Scripture of the Yoginis*) at the beginning of this <term>Cosmic Cycle<note>original: *Kalpa*</note></term> was heard as a bodyless voice from the sky by the gods <term>the Creator, the Preserver, and the Destroyer<note>original: *Brahmâ, Vishnu, and Maheshvara*</note></term>, who were then told to perform <term>Austerities<note>original: *Tapasyâ*—intense spiritual practice or meditation</note></term> for the acquisition of creative and other Powers. It was this <term>Unobstructed Wisdom<note>original: *Aniruddha-sarasvatî*</note></term> who in the <term>Age of Truth<note>original: *Satyayuga*—the first and most spiritual of the four world ages</note></term> appeared in the Heavens before <term>Indra<note>The King of the Gods</note></term> and other proud <term>Deities<note>original: *Devatâs*</note></term> in the form of a brilliant <term>Spirit<note>original: *Yaksha*</note></term>, and crushing the pride of the gods of <term>Fire<note>original: *Agni*</note></term> and <term>Wind<note>original: *Vâyu*</note></term>, in the form of the all-beautiful <term>Umâ<note>A name for the Goddess meaning "Light" or "Mother"</note></term>, taught the <term>Truth of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmatattva*</note></term> to Indra, the King of the Devas <note>This refers to a famous story in the Upanishads—specifically the Kena Upanishad, though the text here cites the Kathopanishad—where the Goddess reveals that the gods' powers actually come from the Absolute</note>.

This <term>Goddess Kâlî<note>The feminine form of Time</note></term> again, who is the <term>Supreme Master<note>original: *Parameshtiguru*</note></term> and grants <term>Final Liberation<note>original: *Kaivalya*</note></term>, taking pity on the sensuous and short-lived <term>Individual Souls<note>original: *Jîvas*</note></term> of the terrible <term>Age of Conflict<note>original: *Kaliyuga*—the current historical age characterized by spiritual decline</note></term>, revealed the <term>Wisdom of Shiva<note>original: *Shâmbhavî Vidyâ*</note></term>. This wisdom, which was taught in the form of conversations between the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> and the <term>Lord<note>original: *Îshvara*</note></term>, had been during the three preceding ages kept as concealed as a lady of high family from public gaze. It contained three sets of sixty-four <term>Scriptures<note>original: *Âgamas*—sacred texts belonging to the Tantric tradition</note></term> each, which revealed the path of Liberation for these souls. Though She is Herself eternal and <term>She whose form is Existence, Consciousness, and Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânandarûpinî*</note></term>, She at times out of compassion for <term>Practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> assumes forms fitted for their <term>Spiritual Practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term>. Similarly the <term>Ancient Knowledge<note>original: *Veda*</note></term>, the Tantric Scriptures, and the rest, though everlasting portions of <term>The Absolute as Sound<note>original: *Shabdabrahmarûpinî*</note></term>, are only revealed to practitioners at different times in the several <term>Ages<note>original: *Yugas*</note></term>.

When the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> who is <term>Pure Consciousness<note>original: *Chinmayî*</note></term> at the beginning of the Cosmic Cycle was pleased by the Austerities of the God...

<summary>This page explains the divine identity of Mahâkâlî as the feminine power (Shakti) of Time and the Absolute. It recounts her historical and mythological appearances—from humbling the gods in the ancient Vedas to revealing the Tantric path of liberation for humanity in the current dark age (Kaliyuga).</summary>

# HYMN TO KÂLÎ

<term>The Howler<note>original: *Rudra*—a fierce form of the god Shiva</note></term>, floating on the Causal Waters, saw the Goddess as She assumed the <term>Universal Aspect<note>original: *Virât*—the physical manifestation of the entire cosmos</note></term> and became visible to Him. At that time, by the command of the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>, the God Rudra saw within the <term>Central Channel<note>original: *Sushumnâ*—the primary spiritual artery in the subtle body</note></term> millions of <term>universes<note>original: *Brahmânda*—literally 'the egg of the creator'</note></term> and millions of Creators (<term>Brahmâs</term>), Preservers (<term>Vishnus</term>), and Destroyers (<term>Maheshvaras</term>) within them. The God, greatly wondering within the Heart-Lotus of the Great Goddess, there saw the <term>Manifestation<note>original: *Mûrtti*</note></term> of the <term>Absolute as Sound<note>original: *Shabdabrahman*</note></term>, consisting of the <term>revealed scriptures<note>original: *Âgamas* and *Nigamas*—the two primary divisions of Tantric revelation</note></term> and other <term>sacred texts<note>original: *Shâstras*</note></term> (See <term>Scripture of the Yoginis<note>original: *Yoginî Tantra*</note></term>, 9th <term>Chapter<note>original: *Patala*</note></term>). He saw that of that Manifestation, the *revealed Tantras* were the <term>Supreme Soul<note>original: *Paramâtmâ*</note></term>, the four <term>Vedas<note>the most ancient Hindu scriptures</note></term> with their <term>auxiliary sciences<note>original: *Angas*</note></term> were the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term>, the six <term>systems of philosophy<note>original: *Darshana*</note></term> were the senses, the <term>Great Ancient Tales<note>original: *Mahâpurânas*</note></term> and <term>Lesser Ancient Tales<note>original: *Upapurânas*</note></term> were the gross body, the <term>Legal Codes<note>original: *Smritis*—remembered traditions</note></term> were the hands and other limbs, and all other sacred texts were the hairs of that great Body. He also saw the fifty <term>Seed-Letters<note>original: *Mâtrikâ*—the creative sounds of the Sanskrit alphabet</note></term> resplendent with <term>Radiant Energy<note>original: *Tejas*</note></term> on the edges and petals of Her Heart-Lotus. Within the center of the Lotus of the <term>Goddess in Her Universal Form<note>original: *Virâtrûpinî*</note></term>, He saw the Tantric revelations, brilliant as millions of suns and moons, filled with all <term>Duty and Righteousness<note>original: *Dharma*</note></term> and <term>Knowledge of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmajnâna*</note></term>, powerful enough to destroy all <term>Illusion<note>original: *Mâyâ*—the power that masks the true nature of reality</note></term>, and full of all <term>Supernatural Powers<note>original: *Siddhis*</note></term> and <term>Supreme Liberation<note>original: *Brahmanirvâna*</note></term>. By the grace of <term>The Great Goddess of Time<note>original: *Mahâkâlî*</note></term>, he fully mastered the Veda, the <term>End of the Vedas<note>original: *Vedânta*—the philosophical conclusion of the scriptures</note></term>, the Ancient Tales, the Legal Codes, and all other sacred texts. Later, the Creator (<term>Brahmâ</term>) and the Preserver (<term>Vishnu</term>) received this knowledge of the Tantric scriptures from Him.

In the <term>Golden Age<note>original: *Satyayuga*—the first and most spiritual age of the world cycle</note></term>, the Creator revealed the Legal Codes, Ancient Tales, and other sacred texts to the <term>Divine Sages<note>original: *Devarshis*</note></term>. In this way, the <term>Science of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term> was spread to the world. This therefore is the authority to show that just as the <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> is everlasting, so too are the Tantric scriptures which speak of the Absolute. Just as in the Golden Age and other ages, only the three <term>twice-born<note>the Brahmin, Kshatriya, and Vaishya classes who undergo spiritual initiation</note></term> classes, who wear the sacred thread, and not the <term>laboring class<note>original: *Shûdra*</note></term> or other lower classes, were entitled to worship according to the Vedas; so too in those three ages only Divine Sages, <term>Brahmin Sages<note>original: *Brahmarshis*</note></term>, and <term>Royal Sages<note>original: *Râjarshis*</note></term>—who had conquered their passions and knew the doctrine of <term>Non-Duality<note>original: *Advaita*</note></term> and the Absolute—were entitled to the Tantric scriptures. These scriptures destroy all sense of difference caused by ignorance and grant knowledge of the <term>Truth of Non-Duality<note>original: *Advaitatattva*</note></term>.

<summary>This page describes a vision granted to the god Shiva (Rudra) wherein the Goddess reveals the entire corpus of sacred scripture as her own cosmic body. It establishes the Tantric Âgamas as eternal truths intended for those who have mastered their passions and seek the realization of non-duality.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

By <term>Shiva’s<note>the god of transformation and the primary teacher of the Tantras</note></term> command, they kept this knowledge as secret in their hearts as they would the knowledge of their own mother’s illicit love. By <term>worship<note>original: *Upâsanâ*—a disciplined approach to devotion or meditation</note></term>, they became <term>liberated while still living<note>original: *Jîvanmukta*—one who has achieved spiritual freedom while still in the body</note></term> and attained <term>merger with the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmanirvâna*</note></term>. At that time, the worship found in the <term>revealed scriptures<note>original: *Âgama*—the Tantric texts</note></term> was unknown to <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> devoted to <term>ritual action<note>original: *Karma*</note></term>. For this reason, many people nowadays think the <term>Tantric scripture<note>original: *Tantrashâstra*</note></term> is of recent origin. Probably all know that in the first three <term>ages of the world<note>original: *Yugas*</note></term>, <term>Brahmin<note>original: *Brâhmana*—the priestly class</note></term> boys, after their initiation with the sacred thread, used to learn the <term>ritual section<note>original: *Karmakânda*</note></term> and the <term>knowledge section<note>original: *Jnânakânda*</note></term> of the <term>Vedas<note>the most ancient holy scriptures of India</note></term> orally from their preceptors. The Veda was not then classified or reduced to writing. Towards the close of the <term>Third Age<note>original: *Dvâparayuga*</note></term>, the Great Sage <term>Vyasa<note>original: *Shrîkrishna-dvaipâyana Maharshi Vedavyâsa*</note></term> divided the Veda into four parts and committed it to writing. This, however, does not show that the Veda is a recent production.

The <term>Supreme Science<note>original: *Parâ vidyâ*</note></term> which is contained in the Tantras was also handed down from generation to generation of <term>Teachers<note>original: *Gurus*</note></term> in the first three ages and is being now similarly transmitted. Towards the end of the Third Age, and at the beginning of the <term>current Dark Age<note>original: *Kali age*</note></term>, merciful Shiva, impelled by compassion for humanity bound in the toils of ignorance, divided the Tantric scripture—which is unlimited knowledge—into three sets of sixty-four parts each, according to the needs of different <term>qualified seekers<note>original: *Adhikârîs*</note></term>. He then told them to <term>Ganesha<note>original: *Ganapati*</note></term> and <term>Kartikeya<note>the god of war</note></term>, the two beloved sons of the Goddess <term>Parvati</term>. They repeated these Tantras to the <term>Sages<note>original: *Rishis*</note></term> of the <term>Hermitages of the Perfected<note>original: *Siddhâshramas*</note></term>, and these last, in their turn, told them to their own disciples. Of the Sages who knew the revealed scriptures, the chief was <term>Dattatreya</term>, an incarnation of <term>Vishnu<note>the god of preservation</note></term>.

At the beginning of the <term>eon<note>original: *Kalpa*</note></term>, the ancient <term>Knowledge of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term> contained in the Tantras appeared from out of the <term>Supreme Teacher<note>original: *Parameshtiguru*</note></term>, who is Himself the Great Knowledge and exists in man’s heart, unlimited and imperishable. If <term>spiritual practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> is done according to the instructions of a <term>true Teacher<note>original: *Sadguru*</note></term>, it becomes visible in the practitioner's heart. <term>Worship<note>original: *Upâsanâ*</note></term> in the Vedic form predominated in the <term>Golden Age<note>original: *Satyayuga*</note></term>. In those days, Brahmins and other <term>twice-born castes<note>the upper three social classes of Hindu society</note></term>, impelled by a desire for wealth, children, and so forth, used to worship <term>Indra<note>Lord of Heaven</note></term>, <term>Agni<note>Fire</note></term>, <term>Vayu<note>Wind</note></term>, <term>Surya<note>the Sun</note></term>, <term>Soma<note>the Moon</note></term>, <term>Varuna<note>Water</note></term>, and other <term>Deities<note>original: *Devas*</note></term> presiding over the forces of nature.

<summary>This page explains that Tantric knowledge was kept strictly secret for ages, leading to the misconception that it is a modern invention. It describes the divine transmission of these texts from Shiva to his sons, and then to various sages, paralleling the oral tradition of the Vedas.</summary>

It establishes that while external religious laws changed across the four ages (Yugas), the internal worship of the supreme Power (Shakti) remained the path of the greatest sages and divine incarnations.</meta>
