...over particular <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*—the active, creative energies of the Divine</note></term> of the <term>Supreme Lord<note>original: *Parameshvara*</note></term> in whom all <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term> reside. But desire-free <term>Sage-seers<note>original: *Brahmarshis* and *Maharshis*—the highest grades of enlightened beings</note></term> performed the <term>spiritual practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> of the <term>Science of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*—the supreme knowledge of the Ultimate Reality</note></term>, which is the full and perfect <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>. And so we see in the tenth book of the <term>Rigveda Collection<note>original: *Rigveda Samhitâ*—the oldest of the four Vedas</note></term> that the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> appeared in the heart of the daughter of the Great Sage <term>Ambhrina</term> and so told the true nature of the <term>Science of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term> to the <term>Seers<note>original: *Rishis*</note></term>. This is the <term>Hymn to the Goddess<note>original: *Devîsûkta*</note></term>, which is full of the <term>principle of non-duality<note>original: *Advaitatattva*—the philosophy that the soul and the divine are one</note></term>, the Hymn telling of the true nature of the <term>Science of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term> in the <term>Veda</term>. 

In the <term>Second Age<note>original: *Tretâ Yuga*</note></term> and other ages, the <term>priestly class<note>original: *Brâhmanas*</note></term> and other "twice-born" members of the upper social classes devoted to the <term>ritual section<note>original: *Karma-kânda*</note></term> of scripture used to perform <term>sacrificial rites<note>original: *Yajnas*</note></term> and so forth, according to the <term>law books<note>original: *Smritishâstras*</note></term> of <term>Manu</term> and others. But the Sage-seer <term>Vashishtha</term> (in the <term>Chinese-style path<note>original: *Chînâchâra*—a specific Tantric ritual path believed to come from the region of the Himalayas/Tibet</note></term>), the King-Sage <term>Vishvâmitra</term> (see the *Celestial Musician's Tantra*, first <term>chapter<note>original: *Patala*</note></term>), King <term>Janaka of Videha</term>, <term>Bhrigurâma</term> the son of <term>Jamadagni</term> (see the *Essence of the Kali Clan*), <term>Lord Ramachandra</term> and other high-souled men were worshippers of the <term>Science of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term>, the full and perfect <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>. 

Again in the <term>Third Age<note>original: *Dvâparayuga*</note></term>, despite the existence of <term>Vedic<note>original: *Vaidik*</note></term> and <term>law-based<note>original: *Smârta*</note></term> cults, the <term>daily fire sacrifice<note>original: *Agnihotra Yajna*</note></term> and other rites used to be performed according to the <term>Ancient Histories<note>original: *Purânas*</note></term>. But the high-souled <term>Lord Krishna<note>original: *Shrîkrishna*</note></term> the son of <term>Vasudeva</term> (see the *Radha Tantra*, the *Goddess Purana*, and the *Mahâbhârata's* *Book of Instructions*, Ch. 14), the five <term>Pandava brothers</term>—namely <term>Yudhishthira</term> and others (see the *Book of Virata*, Ch. 5), the high-souled King-Sage <term>Bhîshma</term>, the great Sage <term>Vedavyâsa</term>, the high-souled <term>Shukadeva</term>, <term>Asita</term>, <term>Devala</term>, and Sage-seers such as <term>Durvâsâ</term> were worshippers of the <term>Great Wisdom<note>original: *Mahâvidyâ*—the ten personified aspects of the Goddess</note></term>, the perfect <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>. Of this, the *Mahâbhârata* and other books provide particular proofs. 

In the present <term>Age of Iron<note>original: *Kaliyuga*—the current era of spiritual decline</note></term> also, the ten <term>sacraments<note>original: *Sangskâras*—rites of passage</note></term> such as marriage and so forth of the twice-born, and the funeral ceremonies such as the <term>ancestor offering<note>original: *Shrâddha*</note></term> are performed according to <term>Vedic<note>original: *Vaidik*</note></term> ritual. <term>Traditional Law<note>original: *Smriti*</note></term> governs the <term>lunar penance<note>original: *Chândrâyana*</note></term> and other matters relating to the <term>stages of life<note>original: *Âshrama*</note></term> and legal affairs such as inheritance. The autumnal <term>worship of Durga<note>original: *Durgâpûjâ*</note></term> and other <term>vowed observances<note>original: *Vratas*</note></term> are performed according to the <term>Ancient Histories<note>original: *Purânas*</note></term>. But initiation, <term>devotional worship<note>original: *Upâsanâ*</note></term> of the <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> with His <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>, and various practices of <term>Yoga<note>spiritual discipline</note></term> are done according to the ritual of the <term>Sacred Tradition<note>original: *Âgama Shâstra*—another name for the Tantras</note></term>.

This latter is of three kinds according to the prevalence of the <term>qualities of nature<note>original: *Gunas*—the three fundamental tendencies: goodness, passion, and darkness</note></term>, namely *Tantra*, *Yâmala*, and *Dâmara*. There are in

<summary>This page tracks the worship of Shakti through history, asserting that while public rituals changed according to the Vedas and Puranas, the greatest sages and heroes of Hindu epic history—such as Krishna, Rama, and Vyasa—were inner practitioners of the Tantric "Science of the Absolute." It classifies modern Hindu life into Vedic sacraments, Puranic festivals, and Tantric (Agama) initiations.</summary>

<page-num>7</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION

...all 192 <term>Sacred Traditions<note>original: *Âgamas*—a collection of scriptures regarded as divinely revealed</note></term> are current, namely 64 each in the regions of <term>Ashvakrântâ</term>, <term>Rathakrântâ</term>, and <term>Vishnukrântâ</term> <note>These refer to three mythological geographical divisions of the Indian subcontinent and surrounding lands</note>. Many <term>Tantras</term> were lost during the Buddhist and <term>Islamic<note>original: *Mahommedan*</note></term> eras, and the few which still remain with <term>spiritual practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> in different parts of the country are not shown by them to anyone except their own disciples, so that these also are on the verge of being lost. The late Rasika Mohana Chattopâdhyâya, with great effort and at great cost, saved some of these, and the English writer <term>Arthur Avalon<note>The pen name of Sir John Woodroffe, a British Orientalist who popularized the study of Tantra in the West</note></term> has done the same; I hope that yet others will be rescued by him in the future.

In the *Yoginî Tantra*, the <term>Lord<note>original: *Îshvara*—here referring to Shiva as the teacher</note></term> says to the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> that the difference between the <term>Vedas</term> and the <term>Sacred Traditions<note>original: *Âgamas*</note></term> is like that between the <term>Individual Soul<note>original: *Jîva*</note></term> and the <term>Supreme Self<note>original: *Âtmâ*</note></term>; that is, between the individual soul covered with <term>Ignorance<note>original: *Avidyâ*</note></term> and the Lord who is full of <term>Divine Knowledge<note>original: *Vidyâ*</note></term>. Indra and other <term>Gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term> who used to be worshipped as Lords in the <term>sacrificial rituals<note>original: *Yajnas*</note></term> held under the <term>Ritual Section<note>original: *Karmakânda*</note></term> or the <term>Collections<note>original: *Samhitâ*</note></term> of the *Vedas* are, in the <term>Tantric Scripture<note>original: *Tantra-shâstra*</note></term>, worshipped as the presiding deities of the <term>Power that protects the directions<note>original: *Dikpâlinî Shakti*</note></term> belonging to Her who is the embodiment of all <term>Powers<note>original: *Sarva-shaktisvarûpinî*</note></term>. The three Lords—<term>Brahmâ</term>, <term>Vishnu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>—of the *Vedas* and the <term>Ancient Chronicles<note>original: *Purânas*</note></term> are, in the *Tantric Scripture*, the presiding deities of the creative, preservative, and dissolving <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term> of the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>. As such, they are worshipped as the supports of the couch of the Great Goddess. She says in the *Devîgîtâ* (*Song of the Goddess*): “Brahmâ, Vishnu, the Lord, and the Eternal Shiva are five <term>Great Corpses<note>original: *Mahâpreta*—a symbolic representation of the gods as inactive matter without the life-giving Power (Shakti) of the Goddess</note></term> at my Feet. They are made of the five <term>Elements<note>original: *Bhûta*</note></term> and represent the five different elements of matter.” “I, however,” She says, “am unmanifested consciousness (*Chit*) and am in every way beyond them.”

Again, the *Veda* says: “All this is verily <term>the Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>.” Despite this <term>Great Statement<note>original: *Mahâvâkya*—one of the core philosophical pillars of the Upanishads</note></term>, various distinctions are made, such as those of caste, the <term>spiritual eligibility<note>original: *Adhikâra*</note></term> of men and women, and so forth. For instance, a male <term>Brahmin<note>original: *Brâhmana*</note></term> may recite <term>Vedic Chants<note>original: *Vaidik Mantras*</note></term>, but Brahmin women may not. Distinctions were again made between objects, such as between the water of the Ganges and the water of a well. All such distinctions are wholly opposed to the spirit of the Great Statement. The *Tantric Scripture* says that the supreme Absolute is both subtle and gross. Depending on the truth of this Great Statement, the <term>Tantric follower<note>original: *Tântrik*</note></term>...

<summary>This page details the preservation of Tantric texts and explains how Tantra reinterprets Vedic deities as subordinate manifestations of the Great Goddess (Mahadevi). It contrasts the egalitarian philosophy of the Tantric "Great Statement" (all is Brahman) with the social and gender-based restrictions found in traditional Vedic practice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological comparison between the Vedic tradition and the Tantric path. It specifically highlights how Tantra challenges orthodox social hierarchies, caste distinctions, and ritual prohibitions in favor of a direct experience of the Divine and the inherent equality of all beings before the Mother of the World.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

# HYMN TO KÂLÎ

<term>Spiritual practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> purify wine which is “not to be taken and drunk” according to the <term>Vedas<note>original: *Veda*—the most ancient Hindu scriptures</note></term>. Considering it to be as holy as nectar, they offer it into the mouth of the <term>internal spiritual power<note>original: *Kulakundalinî*—the divine energy envisioned as a serpent coiled at the base of the spine</note></term> who is <term>Consciousness itself<note>original: *Chitsvarûpinî*</note></term>. Again, in accordance with the <term>Vedas</term>, the <term>Tantric tradition<note>original: *Tantra*—a system of spiritual practices emphasizing the realization of the divine in the material world</note></term> holds food to be sacred and, knowing that food is the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>, ordains the offering of it to the <term>Great God<note>original: *Mahâdeva*—a title for Shiva</note></term>. This offered food is the <term>Great Consecrated Offering<note>original: *Mahâprasâda*</note></term> and is very holy and rare even for the <term>gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term>, regardless of whether it be brought by an <term>outcaste<note>original: *Chandâla*</note></term>, or has even fallen from the mouth of a dog. The <term>Vedas</term> and <term>traditional law codes<note>original: *Smriti*</note></term> say that the <term>outcaste</term> and other low castes are untouchable. On touching them, one must bathe, perform <term>ritual purification<note>original: *Aghamarshana*</note></term>, and so forth. But the <term>Tantric scriptures<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term> say that even an <term>outcaste</term> who has a knowledge of the <term>family doctrine<note>original: *Kula*—the specific lineage or path of Tantra</note></term> and the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> is superior to a <term>priest<note>original: *Brâhmana*</note></term> who does not know the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>. The <term>Tantric scriptures</term> again say that during the <term>ritual circle<note>original: *Chakra*—a gathering for Tantric worship</note></term> all castes are equal. Since all are children of the one Mother of the World, no distinctions should be made at the time of worshipping Her. It is on this <term>Tantric<note>original: *Tântrik*</note></term> authority that no caste distinctions are observed in the matter of eating and so forth in the <term>sacred precinct of the Goddess Vimalâ<note>original: *Virajâkshetra* of *Shrî Shrî Vimalâ Devî*—referring to the temple complex at Puri where social hierarchies are suspended during the distribution of holy food</note></term>. The <term>Vedas</term> again prohibit the performance of <term>sacrifice<note>original: *Yajna*</note></term> or worship after the taking of food. The <term>Tantric scriptures</term>, however, say that one should not worship the <term>Goddess Kâlikâ<note>original: *Kâlikâ*—an epithet of Kali</note></term> whilst suffering from hunger or thirst; otherwise, She becomes angry. That is because <term>God<note>original: *Shiva*</note></term> and the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîva*</note></term> are really one; it is futile to worship the <term>Supreme Soul<note>original: *Paramâtmâ*</note></term> saying ‘I offer this <term>food offering<note>original: *Naivedya*</note></term>’ when the <term>individual soul</term>, who is one with It, is in want of food and drink. <term>Traditional law<note>original: *Smriti*</note></term> again, which explains the <term>Vedas</term>, ordains that the <term>sacred fossil stone<note>original: *Shâlagrama*</note></term> which represents the <term>Lord Vishnu<note>original: *Nârâyana*</note></term> should not be touched or worshipped by any but <term>priests<note>original: *Brâhmanas*</note></term>. On the other hand, the <term>Tantric scriptures</term> ordain that the <term>stone linga<note>original: *Vânalinga*—a naturally formed stone representing the formless aspect of God</note></term> representation of the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> may be touched and worshipped not only by <term>priests</term> but by <term>laborers<note>original: *Shûdras*</note></term>, <term>outcastes<note>original: *Chandâlas*</note></term>, and women. In fact, the <term>ritual section<note>original: *Karmakânda*</note></term> of the <term>Vedas</term> contains many such ordinances opposed to the knowledge of the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>. For this reason, the <term>Lord Sri Krishna<note>original: *Bhagavân Shrîkrishna*</note></term> has said in the *<term>Song of the Lord<note>original: *Gîtâ*—the Bhagavad Gita</note>*</term> that the <term>Vedas</term> are concerned with objects constituted of the <term>three qualities of nature<note>original: *Gunas*—the fundamental forces of Sattva (purity), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia)</note></term> (<term>those whose subject is the three qualities<note>original: *Trigunavishaya*</note></term>) and bids the warrior <term>Arjuna</term> to free himself of the

<summary>This page discusses the egalitarian nature of Tantra, contrasting it with the strict caste and purity laws of the Vedic tradition. It argues that in the eyes of the Mother of the World, all practitioners are equal, and that spiritual knowledge is more important than social status or rigid ritual fasting.</summary>

-># INTRODUCTION<-

<term>Qualities of nature<note>original: *Gunas*—the three fundamental tendencies: *Sattva* (purity), *Rajas* (activity), and *Tamas* (inertia)</note></term>. He says the <term>Vedas<note>original: *Veda*</note></term> contain the <term>ritual section<note>original: *Karmakânda*</note></term> but that he who seeks the <term>state of Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman-state*</note></term> above the <term>Qualities</term> should abandon the <term>ritual section</term> and perform <term>spiritual discipline<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> according to the <term>sacred treatises<note>original: *Shâstra*</note></term> by which Liberation is gained. In spite however of differences in worship and practice both the <term>Vedas</term> and the <term>Tantric treatises<note>original: *Tantra Shâstras*</note></term> are one in holding that there can be no Liberation without the <term>Knowledge of Truth<note>original: *Tattvajnâna*</note></term>. In the <term>Nirvâna Tantra</term> the god <term>Shiva</term> says "Oh <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devi*</note></term>, there is no Liberation without the Knowledge of Truth." According to the <term>Vedas</term>, a <term>spiritual practitioner<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term>, in order to become fit for <term>final liberation<note>original: *Nirvâna*</note></term>, must have first accomplished the fourfold discipline. He must have acquired the faith that the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> is alone everlasting, and have no desire for happiness either on earth or in heaven. He must possess the six virtues, <term>Inner Calm<note>original: *Shama*</note></term>, <term>Self-control<note>original: *Dama*</note></term> and so forth, and must long for Liberation. He then <term>investigates<note>original: *Vichâra*</note></term> and ponders on the <term>Great Saying<note>original: *Mahâvâkya*</note></term> "<term>That thou art<note>original: *Tat tvam asi*—a core Upanishadic teaching asserting the identity of the individual and the Divine</note></term>," and thus realizing the unity of the <term>Supreme Soul<note>original: *Paramâtmâ*</note></term> and the <term>individual soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term>, attains the knowledge "<term>I am He<note>original: *So'ham*</note></term>."

In <term>Tantric worship<note>original: *Tântrik Upâsanâ*</note></term> the <term>knowledge section<note>original: *Jnânakânda*</note></term> is mingled with the <term>ritual section</term>. The <term>Tantric scripture<note>original: *Âgama*</note></term> teaches the ignorant <term>bound soul<note>original: *Pashu*—literally "beast" or "animal," referring to one who lives by instincts and perceives duality</note></term>, steeped in dualism, the <term>heroic path of discipline<note>original: *Vîrabhâva Sâdhanâ*</note></term> in which dualism and non-dualism are mingled. It thus endeavours to raise them to the divine state of <term>those liberated while still living<note>original: *Jîvanmuktas*</note></term>, the state of pure Monism. The lawgiver <term>Manu</term> says "Know dualists to be <term>bound souls</term>. Non-dualists are <term>true seekers of Reality<note>original: *Brâhmanas*</note></term>." The <term>Rudrayâmala<note>a significant Tantric text</note></term> says that the <term>heroic path</term> is revealed for the development of <term>Knowledge<note>original: *Jnâna*</note></term>. After perfecting Knowledge and attainment of the <term>perfection of the Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahmasiddhi*</note></term>, the practitioner becomes a <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> in a pure state of <term>harmony and light<note>original: *Sattva*</note></term>. The <term>Vedânta<note>the philosophical end of the Vedas</note></term> and philosophic scriptures are replete with instructions and arguments touching non-dualism. But they do not indicate the path by which one can be in actual practice non-dualistic. For this reason we see <term>Vedantic scholars<note>original: *Vedântic Pandits*</note></term> deeming it unclean to touch a low caste man such as a <term>laborer<note>original: *Shûdra*</note></term>. They also observe endless distinctions as to what should or should not be eaten, and what should and should not be offered to the <term>Deity</term>. <term>Tantra scripture</term> however says that non-dualistic <term>Feeling<note>original: *Bhâva*</note></term> (<term>non-dualism in feeling<note>original: *Bhâvâdvaita*</note></term>) should be accompanied by non-dualistic—

<term>non-dualism in action<note>original: *Kriyâdvaita*</note></term>. The <term>Yogavâshishtha Râmâyana<note>a philosophical text attributed to the sage Valmiki</note></term> says that to the <term>Sage<note>original: *Muni*</note></term> who realizes <term>non-dualism<note>original: *Advaita*</note></term> in <term>Feeling<note>original: *Bhâva*</note></term>, in <term>Action<note>original: *Kriyâ*</note></term>, and in <term>Objects<note>original: *Dravya*</note></term>—in all these three matters—the world seems but a dream.

According to the instruction of <term>Tantric Scripture<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term>, the <term>spiritual practitioner<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term> rises in the early hours of the morning, and sitting on his bed, meditates as follows: "I am the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> and none other. I am that <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> who knows not grief. I am a form of Being-Consciousness-Bliss, whose true nature is eternal Liberation." Again at noon, sitting at worship, he performs the <term>purification of the elements<note>original: *Bhûtashuddhi*</note></term>, and therein merging the twenty-four <term>principles of existence<note>original: *Tattvas*</note></term> beginning with earth into the <term>Supreme Soul<note>original: *Paramâtmâ*</note></term> and thinking of the <term>Supreme Soul</term> and <term>Individual Soul<note>original: *Jîvâtmâ*</note></term> as one, he meditates: "He I am." The <term>Gandharva Tantra</term> says that, after due obeisance to the <term>Teacher<note>original: *Guru*</note></term>, the wise practitioner should think "He I am" <note>original: *So’ham*</note> and thus unite the individual soul and the Supreme Soul. In all <term>physical meditation<note>original: *Sthûla Dhyâna*</note></term> of the <term>Great Wisdom Goddesses<note>original: *Mahâvidyâs*</note></term>, forming part of daily worship, the Tantric scripture everywhere enjoins meditation on the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> as not different from, but one with, the practitioner’s own <term>Soul<note>original: *Âtmâ*</note></term>. The <term>Kâlî Tantra</term> says that, after meditating as enjoined, the practitioner should worship the Goddess as his own Soul. "He I am" <note>original: *So’ham*</note>. The <term>Kubjikâ Tantra</term> says that the practitioner should think of his Soul as one with Her. The <term>Nîla Tantra</term>, in the meditation on the goddess <term>Târâ</term>, says that meditation should be done on one’s own Soul as one with the <term>Savior-Goddess<note>original: *Târînî*</note></term>. In the <term>Gandharva Tantra</term>, the Great Goddess says, as regards the meditation on <term>Tripurasundarî<note>literally "The Beauty of the Three Cities"</note></term>, that the man who meditates on the unattached, attributeless, and pure Soul which is <term>Tripurâ</term> as one with, and not different from, his own Soul, becomes himself Her <note>original: *Tanmaya*—"composed of that"</note>. One should become Her by ever thinking "She I am" <note>original: *Sâ’ham*</note>. Again, in the <term>Kâlikulasarvasva</term>, the god <term>Shiva</term> says that whoever meditates on the Teacher and recites the Hymn of the spouse of Shiva, and thinks of <term>Kâlikâ’s</term> Soul as one with his own Soul, is the <term>Holy Eternal Shiva<note>original: *Shrî Sadâshiva*</note></term>. Similarly, the <term>Kulârnava Tantra</term> says, "The body is the temple of the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> and the individual soul is the God <term>Eternal Shiva</term>." Let the practitioner give up his ignorance as the <term>discarded offering<note>original: *Nirmâlya*—referring to the remains of an offering which are traditionally removed or thrown away</note></term>, which is thrown

It is not only at times of worship and so forth that the <term>spiritual practitioner<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term> is enjoined to meditate on Her who is the <term>Supreme Soul<note>original: *Paramâtmâ*</note></term> as one with his own <term>inner Soul<note>original: *Âtmâ*</note></term>. <term>Shiva<note>The Aspect of God representing pure consciousness</note></term> teaches that our thought and feeling should be non-dualistic in all that we do, in eating, in walking and so forth. Hence in the <term>Gandharva Tantra</term> Shiva says: "I am both the <term>Deity<note>original: *Deva*</note></term> and the food offered to Him, the flower and perfume and all else. I am the <term>Deity</term>. There is none other than Me. It is I who worship the <term>Deity</term> and I am also the <term>God of Gods<note>original: *Deva of Devas*</note></term>." Again it is ordained that at the time of taking the <term>consecrated wine<note>original: *Kârana*</note></term> and the rest, they should be offered to the Fire of Consciousness in one's own heart, uttering the <term>sacred chant<note>original: *Mantra*</note></term>; and thinking that the <term>coiled spiritual energy<note>original: *Kula-Kundalinî*</note></term> extends to the tip of his tongue, let the <term>practitioner</term> say: "The liquid shines. I am the Light. I am the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>. She I am. I offer this <term>oblation<note>original: *Âhuti*</note></term> to my own Self, <term>Hail!<note>original: *Svâhâ*</note></term>" He who does the <term>spiritual discipline<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> of the <term>Great Wisdom Goddess<note>original: *Mahâvidyâ*</note></term> in the <term>Heroic Path<note>original: *Vîrâchâra*</note></term> with such <term>non-dualistic feeling<note>original: *Advaitabhâva*</note></term> attains by Her Grace to a <term>divine state of being<note>original: *Divyabhâva*</note></term>, and with the thought "I am the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>" becomes liberated whilst living, and on death is one with the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>. In the <term>Devîgîtâ<note>The "Song of the Goddess"</note></term>, the <term>Holy Goddess<note>original: *Shrî Shrî Devî*</note></term> says: "He becomes Myself because both are one." Again the <term>Mahânirvâna Tantra</term> enjoins a similar non-dualistic feeling in the <term>chant</term> to be said when taking the <term>ritual substance<note>original: *Dravya*</note></term>: "The ladle is the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>, the offering is the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>, the fire is the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>, the offering is made by the <term>Ultimate Reality</term> and to the <term>Ultimate Reality</term> he goes who places all his actions in the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>."

The <term>Great Wisdom of Being-Consciousness-Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânanda Mahâvidyâ*</note></term>, in the undistinguishable union of <term>Consciousness<note>original: Shiva</note></term> and <term>Power<note>original: Shakti</note></term>, can alone be worshipped with such non-dualism of feeling. Although <term>Tantric</term> worshippers are divided into five communities—namely <term>Shâkta<note>worshippers of the Goddess</note></term>, <term>Shaiva<note>worshippers of Shiva</note></term>, <term>Vaishnava<note>worshippers of Vishnu</note></term>, <term>Gânapatya<note>worshippers of Ganesha</note></term>, and <term>Saura<note>worshippers of the Sun</note></term>—the first alone includes all <term>twice-born souls<note>original: *Dvijas*; those who have undergone spiritual initiation</note></term>, since all worshippers of <term>Sâvitrî<note>the Gâyatrî mantra</note></term>, the Mother of the <term>Veda</term>, belong to the <term>Shâkta</term> community. The <term>Mâtrikâbheda Tantra</term> says: "<term>Sâvitrî</term>, the Mother of the <term>Veda</term>, was born of the sweat of <term>Kâlî's</term> body. That <term>Goddess</term> grants the threefold fruit <note>the goals of virtue, prosperity, and enjoyment</note> and is the <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> of the <term>Ultimate Reality</term>." <term>Practitioners</term> belonging to the other four communities worship their respective

<summary>This page explains the Tantric practice of "Advaitabhâva" (non-dual feeling), where the practitioner identifies themselves entirely with the Deity during rituals and daily life. It argues that all initiated Hindus are essentially Shâktas (Goddess-worshippers) because the fundamental Vedic prayer, the Gâyatrî, originates from the Goddess Kâlî.</summary>

It transitions from the previous page's focus on the practitioner's self-identification with the Goddess to a broader theological argument for the unity of the Mother and Father aspects of the Divine.</meta>

male <term>Deities<note>original: *Devatâs*</note></term> associating with them their <term>Feminine Energies<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term>. Thus the <term>worshippers of Shiva<note>original: *Shaivas*</note></term> worship Shiva under the names Umâ-Maheshvara, Shiva-Durgâ, Kâlî-Shangkara, <term>Arddhanârîshvara<note>The Lord who is half-woman, depicting the union of Shiva and Parvati in one body</note></term> and so forth. The <term>worshippers of Vishnu<note>original: *Vaishnavas*</note></term> worship Vishnu under the names, Râdhâ-Krishna, Lakshmî-Nârâyana, Sîtâ-Râma, Shrî-Hari and so forth. In the *Nirvâna Tantra* <term>Shrî Krishna<note>A major deity and incarnation of Vishnu</note></term> says: "To those who perform the <term>repetitive prayer<note>original: *Japa*</note></term> of Râdhâ first and then Krishna, to such I, of a surety, grant a happy lot even now and here." By uttering the name Sîtâ-Râma (Sîtâ coming first) one utters the <term>saving mantra<note>original: *Târa*</note></term> of the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>, and for this reason it is also called the <term>Deliverer-Brahman<note>original: *Târaka-Brahma*</note></term>. The <term>Sun-worshippers<note>original: *Sauras*</note></term> perform their worship with the <term>sacred chant<note>original: *Mantra*</note></term>: "Obeisance to the <term>Holy Sun<note>original: *Shrî Sûryya*</note></term> accompanied by the <term>Energy<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> who reveals." 

Moreover the <term>Seed-syllable of Illusion<note>original: *Mâyâ-Bîja*—the sound *Hrîm*</note></term>, which is the <term>sacred syllable<note>original: *Pranava*</note></term> of the Goddess, is added to the <term>root-mantra<note>original: *Mûlamantra*</note></term> by every sect. This clearly shows that all these five sects are directly or indirectly worshippers of the <term>Ultimate Reality<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> who is the <term>Union of Shiva and Shakti<note>original: *Shivashaktyâtmaka*</note></term> both in his <term>attribute-less<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term> and <term>attribute-possessing<note>original: *Saguna*</note></term> aspects. The *Kaivalyopanishad* says: "By meditation on the three-eyed, blue-throated serene Lord <term>Parameshvara<note>The Supreme Lord Shiva</note></term>, who is without beginning, middle and end, who is one and pervades all things, who is wonderful, <term>Consciousness-Bliss<note>original: *Chidânanda*</note></term> Itself, accompanied by the Goddess Umâ, the <term>Sage<note>original: *Muni*</note></term> goes to the <term>Source of all being<note>original: *Bhûtayoni*</note></term>, to the Witness of all, who is beyond all darkness." 

Hence in the <term>Tantric Scripture<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term>, Shiva has said that the <term>Shiva-Shakti Principle<note>original: *Tattva*</note></term> is the cause of the <term>Knowledge of Reality<note>original: *Tattva-jnâna*</note></term> and therefore <term>prayer<note>original: *Japa*</note></term> should be done by a mantra in which they are united. That is, one attains the knowledge of reality, which is liberation, by worshipping the Ultimate as Mother and Father. All mantras being composed of Shiva and Shakti, one should meditate on Shiva-Shakti as being one. In the Tantric Scripture also Shiva has said that there is no difference between them, for they are in an <term>inseparable relationship<note>original: *Avinâbhâvasambandha*</note></term>. He who is Shiva is also the Energy (Shakti), and She who is the Energy is also Shiva. Fatherhood and Motherhood are merely distinctions of name. In reality they stand for one and the same thing. The Tantric Scripture again says that Energy, the Great Lord, and the Ultimate Reality all denote the...

<summary>This page explains that all major Hindu sects (Shaiva, Vaishnava, Saura) recognize the inseparable union of male and female divine principles (Shiva and Shakti). It argues that spiritual liberation and true knowledge come from worshipping the Divine as both Father and Mother, as they are ultimately one and the same reality.</summary>

While the previous page discussed how different sects (Shaiva, Vaishnava, Saura) worship the union of male and female aspects, this page clarifies that these gendered distinctions are purely linguistic and functional for the practitioner, rather than inherent to the ultimate, formless Reality.</meta>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

same Being. Male, female, and neuter are verbal and not real distinctions. <term>Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>, the <term>Great Lord<note>original: *Maheshvara*</note></term>, and the <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term>; all three denote the one eternal <term>Supreme Wisdom<note>original: *Mahâvidyâ*</note></term> who is <term>Being, Consciousness, and Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânanda*</note></term>. Although the Supreme Wisdom is in truth <term>without attributes<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term> and eternal, She assumes various <term>manifested forms<note>original: *Mâyik*</note></term>, varying according to the <term>universal qualities<note>original: *Gunas*; the three fundamental forces of nature: harmony, passion, and inertia</note></term>, for the fulfillment of the desires of <term>spiritual practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term>. It is said in the <term>*Chandî*<note>A foundational text of Goddess worship, also known as the *Devi Mahatmya*</note></term> that She ever appears to fulfill the purposes of the <term>Gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term>, and at such times She, who is Truth eternal, is commonly said to be generated. In the *Devyâgama* scripture it is said: "The <term>Great Illusion<note>original: *Mahâmâyâ*</note></term> who is <term>formed of consciousness<note>original: *Chitirûpâ*</note></term> and <term>whose nature is the Supreme Absolute<note>original: *Parabrahmasvarûpinî*</note></term> assumes various forms by Her grace toward practitioners." We may meditate on the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> as either female or male, for these terms may be attributed to any physical body. They cannot, however, be attributed to Her insofar as She is Being, Consciousness, and Bliss. Practitioners of the way of Power worship the Absolute as **Mother**, for in the world, the mother-aspect alone of Her who is the Absolute is fully manifested. In the *Yâmala* text, the god <term>Shiva<note>The destroyer and regenerator within the Hindu trinity, often depicted as the Great Ascetic</note></term> says:—

"My Beloved, the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> may be thought of as female or male, or the <term>One whose form is Being-Consciousness-Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânandarûpinî*</note></term> may be thought of as the <term>Partless Absolute<note>original: *Nishkala Brahman*</note></term>. But in truth, She is neither a female, male, nor neuter being, nor an inanimate thing. But like the term <term>*Kalpavatî*<note>original: *Kalpavatî*; a word in the feminine gender used to denote a tree</note></term>, feminine terms are attributed to Her."

In fact, the main cause of the birth and nourishment of men and animals is their respective mothers. Their fathers are merely <term>helpers<note>original: *Sahakârî*</note></term>. Every <term>living soul<note>original: *Jîva*</note></term>, on issuing from his mother’s womb, lives on her milk and receives his first initiation with the <term>Sacred Sound<note>original: *Mantra*</note></term> "**Mâ**" (Mother). The <term>first preceptor<note>original: *Âdiguru*</note></term> of every man is his mother. She is his visible <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term>. His first lessons are learned from her. It is also the characteristic of the Earth to generate and nourish all living beings, like a mother, by producing for them all kinds of fruits and grains and holding them in her bosom. Hence we are not wrong in saying that the world is full of the Mother.

In mathematics, zero has no value and is merely an empty, <term>formless<note>original: *Nirâkâra*</note></term> thing, indicative of infinity until it is joined to an integer. But when joined to the figure 1, it converts it into

<summary>This page explains that the Divine is ultimately beyond gender and form, but is worshipped as the "Mother" because the maternal role of generation and nourishment is the most visible aspect of the Absolute in the physical world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Goddess from page 13. While the previous page established that the Divine is a single, formless Reality (Brahman) that assumes gendered forms for the sake of the devotee, this page details the specific manifestations known as the Ten Great Wisdoms (Dashamahâvidyâ) and how they are categorized by their cosmic qualities (Gunas) and lineage groups (Kulas).</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

10. Similarly, when She who is the <term>formless Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> is joined to Her own <term>Primordial Nature<note>original: *Prakriti*</note></term>—consisting of the <term>three universal qualities<note>original: *Gunas*</note></term> and described in <term>revealed scripture<note>original: *Shruti*; the Vedas</note></term> as "the unborn one, red, black, and white"—She then assumes ten different forms for the fulfillment of the <term>practitioner's<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term> desires. These are the <term>Ten Great Wisdoms<note>original: *Dashamahâvidyâ*</note></term>, whose variety arises from the different proportions of the three universal qualities. These Ten Great Wisdoms are the union of <term>Consciousness and Power<note>original: *Shiva and Shakti*</note></term>. These ten forms are Kâlî and Târâ, the Great Wisdom Shodashî, Bhuvaneshvarî, Bhairavî, Chhinnamastâ, Dhûmâvatî, the Wisdom Bagalâ, the <term>Wisdom of Success<note>original: *Siddhavidyâ*</note></term> Mâtanggî, and Kamalâ. Some <term>scriptures<note>original: *Tantras*</note></term> mention eighteen Great Wisdoms, but these are merely variations of the primary ten.

Of the Ten Great Wisdoms, Kâlî is <term>primarily composed of pure harmony<note>original: *Shuddhasattvagunapradhânâ*</note></term>, <term>changeless<note>original: *Nirvikârâ*</note></term>, and <term>the manifest nature of the attribute-less Absolute<note>original: *Nirgunabrahmasvarûpaprakâshikâ*</note></term>. It is this primordial form alone that directly grants <term>supreme liberation<note>original: *Kaivalya*</note></term>. In the *Yoginî Tantra*, the Goddess says: "Now see my form which is the <term>bliss of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmânanda*</note></term> and supreme. Listen, this form is the <term>highest state<note>original: *Paramadhâma*</note></term> in the form of Kâlî. There is no form of the Absolute higher than this." In the *Kâmadhenu Tantra*, Shiva says: "In the void is Kâlî who grants supreme liberation." 

Târâ is <term>composed of the quality of harmony<note>original: *Sattvagunâtmikâ*</note></term> and <term>the giver of the wisdom of reality<note>original: *Tattvavidyâdâyinî*</note></term>. Shodashî (also known as the <term>Great Beautiful One of the Three Cities<note>original: *Mahâtripurasundarî*</note></term>), Bhuvaneshvarî, and Chhinnamastâ are <term>primarily composed of passion<note>original: *Rajah-pradhânâ*</note></term> and the quality of harmony; therefore, they grant <term>secondary liberation<note>original: *Gaunamukti*</note></term> in the form of <term>Heaven<note>original: *Svarga*</note></term>, <term>divine power<note>original: *Aishvaryya*</note></term>, and so forth. The forms of Dhûmâvatî, Bagalâ, Mâtanggî, and Kamalâ are <term>primarily composed of inertia<note>original: *Tamah-pradhâna*</note></term>, and thus their <term>spiritual practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> is performed in the <term>six magical acts<note>original: *Shatkarma*; these include rituals for protection, attraction, or the destruction of enemies</note></term>, such as causing death to others and so forth. In short, all ten forms of the Great Goddess give both worldly enjoyment and spiritual liberation, either directly or indirectly.

The forms of the Great Wisdom are divided into two groups: the Lineage of Kâlî and the Lineage of Lakshmi. <note>original: *Kâlîkula* and *Shrîkula*</note> Thus the *Niruttara Tantra* says: "Kâlî, Târâ, Red Kâlî, Bhuvanâ, Marddinî, Triputâ, Tvaritâ, Durgâ, and the Wisdom Pratyangirâ belong to the **Lineage of Kâlî**. And to the **Lineage of Lakshmi** belong Sundarî, Bhairavî, Bâlâ, Bagalâ, Kamalâ, Dhûmâvatî, Mâtanggî, the Wisdom Svapnâvatî, and the Great Wisdom Madhumatî. Of all the Wisdoms of Success, <term>the South-facing One<note>original: *Dakshinâ*; referring to Dakshinakâlî</note></term> is, O my beloved, the <term>Source<note>original: *Prakriti*</note></term>."

<summary>This page describes the Ten Mahâvidyâs (Wisdom Goddesses), explaining how their different forms arise from the three universal qualities (Gunas) to suit different spiritual goals. It also categorizes these goddesses into two primary lineages: the Kâlî-kula and the Shrî-kula.</summary>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

The <term>Lineage of Kali<note>original: *Kâlî-kula*</note></term> is intended for the worship of <term>seekers of knowledge<note>original: *Jnânîs*</note></term> who are in the <term>divine<note>original: *Divya*</note></term> and <term>heroic<note>original: *Vîrabhâva*</note></term> <term>dispositions<note>original: *Bhâvas*; the internal temperament or spiritual state of a practitioner</note></term>, while the <term>Lineage of Fortune<note>original: *Shrî-kula*</note></term> is for the worship of <term>those on the path of ritual action<note>original: *Karmins*</note></term> in the divine, heroic, and <term>ordinary<note>original: *Pashu*</note></term> dispositions. The <term>Tantric scriptures<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term> give an account of the <term>sacred chants<note>original: *Mantras*</note></term>, <term>meditation diagrams<note>original: *Yantras*</note></term>, the mode of worship, and so forth for all the ten or eighteen <term>Great Wisdoms<note>original: *Mahâvidyâs*</note></term>. But almost all <term>Tantric<note>original: *Tântrik*</note></term> writings hymn the greatness of, and give the highest place to, <term>Kali<note>original: *Kâlikâ*</note></term>, the first Great Wisdom, for the others are but different forms of <term>Kali, whose nature is the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmarûpinî Kâlikâ*</note></term>. The <term>Wish-Fulfilling Tree of Revealed Scripture<note>original: *Nigama Kalpataru*</note></term> says: "Of all castes, the <term>priest<note>original: *Brâhmana*</note></term> is the highest. Amongst all <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term>, the <term>worshiper of the Goddess<note>original: *Shâkta*</note></term> is the highest. Of these worshipers, he is the chief who performs the <term>repetitive prayer<note>original: *Japa*</note></term> of the <term>chant of Kali<note>original: *Kâlîmantra*</note></term>." The <term>Pichchhila Tantra</term> also says: "Of all the sacred chants of the <term>gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term>, that of Kali is the best. Even the most wretched can become <term>liberated while still living<note>original: *Jîvanmukta*</note></term> simply through this chant." In the <term>Yogini Tantra</term>, the god <term>Shiva</term> says: "This Wisdom that is Kali is the Great-Great-Great Wisdom, through whom even the worst may attain <term>final liberation<note>original: *Nirvâna*</note></term>. Even the <term>Creator<note>original: *Brahmâ*</note></term>, the <term>Preserver<note>original: *Vishnu*</note></term>, and the <term>Destroyer<note>original: *Maheshvara*</note></term> are Her worshipers. She who is Kali, the supreme Wisdom, is <term>the Savior<note>original: *Târâ*</note></term> also. The notion of a difference between them has given rise to various different chants." Again, the <term>Kamakhya Tantra</term> says: "Oh <term>Supreme Goddess<note>original: *Parameshvari*</note></term>, seven hundred thousand Great Wisdoms remain hidden. Of them all, <term>the Goddess of Sixteen<note>original: *Shodashî*</note></term> is said to be the most sublime. But Oh <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devi*</note></term>, Mother of the World, Kali is the mother even of Her." The <term>Niruttara Tantra</term> says: "Without knowledge of the <term>Divine Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term>, Oh Goddess, there is no final liberation. That Power is <term>Kali of the South<note>original: *Dakshinâ Kâlî*</note></term>, who is the very form of all wisdoms (<term>the embodiment of all wisdom<note>original: *Sarvvavidyârûpinî*</note></term>)." The <term>Yamala</term> scripture again says: "As is Kali, so is the Savior, and so are the <term>Severed-Headed Goddess<note>original: *Chhinnâ*</note></term> and <term>Kulluka</term>. Oh Goddess, you, who are the supreme Kali, are also the <term>sacred form<note>original: *Mûrtti*</note></term> which is composed of these four." In the <term>Vedic<note>original: *Vaidik*</note></term> system, the <term>fire-maintaining priests<note>original: *Sâgnika Brâhmanas*</note></term> achieved their ends by the offering of oblations to the *seven lolling tongues of fire* named <term>The Black One<note>original: *Kâlî*</note></term>, <term>The Terrible One<note>original: *Karâlî*</note></term>, <term>The Swift-as-Thought<note>original: *Manojavâ*</note></term>, <term>The Deep Red One<note>original: *Sulohitâ*</note></term>, <term>The Smoke-Colored One<note>original: *Sudhûmravarnâ*</note></term>, <term>The Scattering of Sparks<note>original: *Sphulinginî*</note></term>, and the <term>Goddess of All-Forms<note>original: *Devî Vishvaruchi*</note></term> (from the 1st <term>Seven-fold section<note>original: *Saptaka*</note></term>, 2nd <term>Chapter<note>original: *Khanda*</note></term>, 4th <term>Aphorism<note>original: *Sûtra*</note></term>).

Another important characteristic of the Tantric scripture remains to be mentioned. Although this scripture is very liberal in matters of practice and worship and does not recognize

distinctions of caste and so forth, it has yet repeatedly enjoined <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> to keep this <term>way of conduct<note>original: *Âchâra*</note></term> hidden from ignorant <term>ordinary men<note>original: *Pashus*; literally "animals," referring to those bound by social conventions and worldly ignorance</note></term>. Of <term>Kaulas<note>advanced practitioners of the Kula path</note></term> it says that ‘they are at heart <term>Shakti-worshippers<note>original: *Shâktas*</note></term>, outwardly <term>Shiva-worshippers<note>original: *Shaivas*</note></term>, and in gatherings <term>Vishnu-worshippers<note>original: *Vaishnavas*</note></term>’. In the *Gandharva Tantra*, Shiva says that only such men as are without dualism, have controlled their passions and are devoted to the <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> are entitled to this <term>scripture<note>original: *Shâstra*</note></term>. ‘He alone is entitled, who is a believer, pure, self-controlled, without dualism who lives in the Absolute, speaks of the Absolute, is devoted to the Absolute, takes refuge in the Absolute, who is free from all feeling of enmity against others, and who is ever engaged in doing good to all beings. Others are not true practitioners (<term>false practitioners<note>original: *Bhramasâdhaka*</note></term>). It should not be told to the uninitiated, to those who are insincere, or to men of shallow knowledge.’ 

For this reason Shiva has used symbols in the teaching of all <term>meditative visualizations<note>original: *Dhyânas*</note></term>, <term>sacred chants<note>original: *Mantras*</note></term>, <term>geometric diagrams<note>original: *Yantras*</note></term>, and modes of <term>spiritual practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> of <term>Gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term> and <term>Goddesses<note>original: *Devîs*</note></term>. The meaning of these symbols is not known to any but the <term>true teacher<note>original: *Sadguru*</note></term>. Hence the secret mysteries are unintelligible even to the learned without the grace of the Guru. In the *Kulârnava Tantra*, Shiva says ‘There are many teachers who know the Vedas, the scriptures and so forth. But, Oh Devi, rare is the teacher who knows the meaning of the supreme <term>Truth<note>original: *Tattva*</note></term>’. Hence in order to know the true meaning of the visualizations and so forth, there is no other means than to seek refuge with the teacher who knows the meaning of all <term>revealed scriptures<note>original: *Âgamas*</note></term>.

It is owing to ignorance of the true nature of the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> that even <term>Knowledge of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ*</note></term>, who is subtler than the most subtle and Consciousness Itself, seems to be a gross thing. Even learned men do not shrink from saying that this <term>Goddess who is the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmamayî*</note></term>, whose desires are fully realized (<term>completely fulfilled<note>original: *Pûrnakâmâ*</note></term>) is fond of offerings of blood, flesh and so forth <note>The author argues that literal interpretations of "blood and flesh" miss the internal, symbolic meaning of these Tantric offerings</note>. In the *Jnânasangkalinî Tantra*, Shiva says, ‘Fire is the <term>deity<note>original: *Deva*</note></term> of the <term>twice born<note>original: *Dvija*; the members of the three upper social classes who have undergone spiritual initiation</note></term>. The deity of <term>Sages<note>original: *Munis*</note></term> is in

# INTRODUCTION

their hearts. Men of small intelligence worship images. To the wise, the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> is everywhere. That is, <term>ritual-focused priests<note>original: *Karmin Brâhmanas*</note></term> worship <term>Fire<note>original: *Agni*</note></term> as the <term>Lord<note>original: *Îshvara*</note></term>, <term>practitioners of yoga<note>original: *Yogîs*</note></term> see the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> in their own hearts, men of small intelligence (that is, compared with the others) worship the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term> in images, and high-souled seers of the <term>Truth<note>original: *Tattva*</note></term> see the <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> everywhere. In fact, much as a teacher shows his little students small globes and maps in order to make them understand the nature of the great earth, so <term>Teachers<note>original: *Gurus*</note></term> counsel <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> of no great intelligence and of inferior <term>spiritual competence<note>original: *Adhikâra*</note></term> to meditate on <term>physical<note>original: *Sthûla*</note></term> forms in images and pictures so that their wandering minds may be rested, and they may learn the true aspects of the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term>. 

Unfortunately, however, ignorant men consider the <term>physical<note>original: *Sthûla*</note></term> form to be the true aspect of the <term>Deity<note>original: *Devatâ*</note></term>. In the *Kulârnava Tantra*, <term>Shiva<note>The supreme teacher in this tradition</note></term> says that some meditate on the <term>physical<note>original: *Sthûla*</note></term> to still the mind, which, when so stilled, can fix itself on the <term>subtle<note>original: *Sûkshma*</note></term>. The <term>practitioner<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term> should first learn from the <term>Teacher<note>original: *Guru*</note></term> what quality or action each limb of the image represents, and should then practice meditation on the subtle; otherwise, the gross form will itself become for him mere earth or stone. In the *Kubjikâ Tantra*, <term>Shiva<note>original: *Shiva*</note></term> says: "Oh Lady of <term>the Great Lord<note>original: *Mahesha*</note></term>, one should meditate on the Formless (here used in the sense as opposed to forms of images, etc.) along with the form. It is by constant practice, Oh <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devi*</note></term>, that one realizes the formless."

Hence, <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> who desire Liberation should always think of the <term>essential nature of the truth<note>original: *Svarûpatattva*</note></term> of <term>Kali as the Wisdom of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmavidyâ Kâlikâ*</note></term>. Of this <term>true nature<note>original: *Svarûpa*</note></term>, the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> says in the *Mahâbhâgavata*: 

> "Those who long for Liberation should, in order to gain freedom from the bonds of the body, meditate on that <term>aspect<note>original: *Rûpa*</note></term> of Mine which is the supreme <term>Light<note>original: *Jyotih*</note></term>, <term>subtle<note>original: *Sûkshma*</note></term>, and <term>indivisible<note>original: *Nishkala*</note></term>, <term>attribute-less<note>original: *Nirguna*</note></term>, the all-pervading unbeginning, non-dual sole Cause which is <term>Existence-Consciousness-Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânanda*</note></term> Itself. This is the <term>true nature<note>original: *Svarûpa*</note></term> of the <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> which is beyond all mind and speech."

The *Mârkandeya Purâna* says: 

> "The <term>Great Power of Illusion<note>original: *Mahâmâyâ*</note></term> is <term>indivisible<note>original: *Nishkalâ*</note></term>, <term>attribute-less<note>original: *Nirgunâ*</note></term>, endless, undecaying, unthinkable, formless and both..."

<sig>3</sig>

<summary>This page explains that while the Absolute is formless and all-pervading, physical images are used as educational tools for practitioners with less spiritual experience to steady their minds. It emphasizes that advanced meditation should eventually move beyond physical forms to the "subtle" and "attribute-less" nature of the Goddess, which is pure consciousness.</summary>

# HYMN TO KÂLÎ

eternal (<term>Eternal<note>original: *Nityâ*</note></term>) and transient (<term>Transient<note>original: *Anityâ*</note></term>)', that is, <term>the Great Illusory Power of Kali<note>original: *Mahâmâyâ Kâlikâ*</note></term> is free from <term>limitation<note>original: *Kalâ* or *Mâyâ*</note></term> and free from the <term>three qualities of nature<note>original: *Gunas*</note></term>, without end, imperishable, eternal, and not transient like the <term>world<note>original: *Jagat*</note></term>. She is formless, and therefore as such, She is not the object of meditation. In the <term>Purana of the Tortoise<note>original: *Kûrma Purâna*</note></term>, <term>Vishnu<note>the Preserver god</note></term> in the form of a Tortoise says that the Supreme <term>Goddess<note>original: *Devî*</note></term> is <term>without attributes<note>original: *Nirgunâ*</note></term>, pure, white, stainless, free from all duality, and realizable by the <term>Soul<note>original: *Âtmâ*</note></term> only. This state of Hers is attainable only by <term>spiritual wisdom<note>original: *Jnâna*</note></term>. In the <term>Tantra of the Giver of Desires<note>original: *Kâmadâ Tantra*</note></term>, <term>Shiva<note>the Destroyer god</note></term> says, 'That eternal Kali who is the supreme <term>Absolute<note>original: *Brahman*</note></term> is one without a second, whether male or female. She has neither form, <term>physical support<note>original: *Âdhâra*</note></term>, nor <term>limiting condition<note>original: *Upâdhi*</note></term>. She is sinless and imperishable <term>Existence-Consciousness-Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânanda*</note></term>, the Great Absolute.' She who is the eternal Absolute has neither <term>appearance<note>original: *Âvirbhâva*</note></term> nor <term>disappearance<note>original: *Tirobhâva*</note></term>, and being all-pervading, She cannot be said, like other <term>gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term> and <term>goddesses<note>original: *Devîs*</note></term>, to reside in any particular <term>realm<note>original: *Loka*</note></term>. Thus, the Creator resides in the <term>World of Brahma<note>original: *Brahmaloka*</note></term>, Vishnu in the <term>World of Vishnu<note>original: *Vishnuloka*</note></term>, Rudra in <term>Mount Kailasa<note>original: *Kailâsa*</note></term>, and Lord Krishna in the <term>World of Cows<note>original: *Goloka*</note></term>, but the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> is always and everywhere equally present; though for the fulfillment of the desires of <term>practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term>, She appears in particular forms in their minds and hearts. It is clear, therefore, that her <term>physical<note>original: *Sthûla*</note></term> aspect is <term>made of illusion<note>original: *Mâyâmaya*</note></term> and transient (<term>Transient<note>original: *Anitya*</note></term>). For this reason, Shiva, in the <term>Gandharva Tantra<note>an ancient scripture on ritual and music</note></term>, says, 'That <term>form<note>original: *Rûpa*</note></term> of the Goddess which is the Supreme Bliss and the Great Cause of the worlds neither appears nor disappears.' In the <term>Kulârnava Tantra<note>a foundational text of the Kaula school</note></term>, Shiva says, 'It neither rises nor sets, nor grows nor decays; It shines by Itself and makes others shine without any help. This aspect is <term>without condition<note>original: *Anavasthâ*</note></term> and is <term>pure existence<note>original: *Sattâmâtrâ*</note></term> and <term>unknowable to the senses<note>original: *Agochara*</note></term>.' That is, the <term>essential nature<note>original: *Svarûpa*</note></term> aspect of the Great Goddess who is Supreme Bliss is the root-cause of this world of three qualities. This aspect has no appearance or disappearance and no growth or decay. 'It is self-manifest and manifests all other objects. It is beyond the states of waking, dreams, and sleep. It is unattainable by speech and mind and is Being itself.'

In fact, just as fire which, though pervading all objects, does not show its power of burning and lighting, and can-

<summary>This page explains that while the Goddess Kali appears in physical forms for the benefit of worshippers, her true nature is the formless, eternal, and all-pervading Absolute (Brahman). Citing various Tantras and Puranas, the text emphasizes that she transcends the specific divine realms inhabited by other deities like Brahma or Vishnu.</summary>

Just as fire exists potentially in wood but requires friction to manifest, the Goddess is all-pervading but requires spiritual discipline to be perceived. The catchword "tattva" from the previous page's footer completes the term "Panchatattva" (The Five Elements/Ritual Ingredients) at the end of this page.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

not be put to use for cooking and so forth, until it has been generated by the friction of two objects, so although <term>She who is Full of Consciousness<note>original: *Chinmayî*</note></term> is all-pervading, She does not become visible nor does She grant one's desire without the action of <term>Spiritual Practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*; the disciplined path of a seeker</note></term>. Again just as the Sun itself, motionless in the distant Heavens, by its rays draws moisture from the earth, so <term>the Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term>, who is the abode of all <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term>, though in Her-self <term>Changeless<note>original: *Nirvikârâ*</note></term> creates (and the like) the world by means of the eight <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term>, <term>The Power of the Creator<note>original: *Brahmânî*</note></term>, <term>The Power of the Preserver<note>original: *Vaishnavî*</note></term>, <term>The Power of the Great Lord<note>original: *Mâheshvarî*</note></term> and other <term>Deities<note>original: *Devatâs*</note></term>, presiding as Her creative and other <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term>. For this reason in the <term>Sacred Diagram<note>original: *Yantra*; a geometric tool for meditation</note></term> of <term>The Great Goddess Kali<note>original: *Mahâdevî Kâlikâ*</note></term> (see the <term>Secret Wisdom of Kali<note>original: *Kâlikopanishad*</note></term>) the <term>Practitioner<note>original: *Sâdhaka*</note></term> worships the fifteen <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term> of <term>The Black One<note>original: *Kâlî*</note></term> and others in the fifteen corners, the eight <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term> of <term>The Creative Force<note>original: *Brâhmî*</note></term> and others on the eight petals, the eight <term>Terrible Guardians<note>original: *Bhairavas*</note></term> and <term>Youthful Protectors<note>original: *Vatukas*</note></term> such as <term>He of the Black Limbs<note>original: *Asitângga*</note></term> and the rest at the edges of the eight petals, the four <term>Deities<note>original: *Devatâs*</note></term>, <term>The Preserver<note>original: *Vishnu*</note></term> and others, at the four corners of the <term>Sacred Diagram<note>original: *Yantra*</note></term>, and the ten <term>Guardians of the Directions<note>original: *Dikpâlas*</note></term>, <term>The King of Gods<note>original: *Indra*</note></term> and others, in the ten directions as being the rays of <term>The Black One<note>original: *Kâlikâ*</note></term> who is Herself a <term>Mass of Pure Radiance<note>original: *Tejoghana*</note></term>. <term>The Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> is worshipped as the <term>Form<note>original: *Mûrtti*</note></term> consisting of <term>The Union of Consciousness and Power<note>original: *Shivashaktimaya*</note></term> in the <term>Central Point<note>original: *Bindu*</note></term> at the centre of the <term>Sacred Diagram<note>original: *Yantra*</note></term>.

Although the <term>Tantric Scripture<note>original: *Âgama Shâstra*</note></term>, which grants the <term>State of Non-Duality<note>original: *Advaitabhâva*</note></term> and educes <term>Knowledge of Reality<note>original: *Tattvajnâna*</note></term>, has been revealed by the all-merciful <term>Holy Terrible One and His Consort<note>original: *Shrî Shrî Bhairava and Bhairavî*</note></term>, it is still unknown to a mass of people. Many in fact to-day despise the <term>Tantra<note>the body of esoteric scriptures and practices</note></term> because it contains <term>The Hero's Path<note>original: *Vîrâchâra*</note></term> and <term>The Noble Tradition<note>original: *Kulâchâra*</note></term>, and some even refuse to admit that it is a <term>Sacred Law<note>original: *Dharmashâstra*</note></term> at all. If they had read the <term>Tantra Scripture<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term> intelligently and learned its principles from <term>Practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> truly versed in it, they would have realized how mistaken were their notions of it and, instead of despising it, would certainly have admitted that this <term>Scripture<note>original: *Shâstra*</note></term> is the only means of Liberation for the undisciplined, weakminded and short-lived. Seeing that wine, flesh, fish are consumed and sexual intercourse takes place in the world at large I am myself unable to under-stand why many people should shudder at the <term>Spiritual Practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> of the <term>Five Elements<note>original: *Panchatattva*; often referred to as the 'Five Ms', these are ritual ingredients used in certain Tantric ceremonies</note></term>

<summary>This page explains that the Goddess, while all-pervading and changeless, manifests through specific spiritual practices and is visualized within the geometric structure of a Yantra. The author also defends Tantric scriptures against modern critics, arguing they are essential for liberation in the current age despite their controversial rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual use of the "Five Ms" (Pancha-ma-kâra), which began on the previous page. The text moves from a general discussion of spiritual practice to a specific defense of Tantric rituals that use wine and meat, framing them as controlled medicinal remedies for the spiritual ailments of the current age.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

# HYMN TO KÂLÎ

<term>the Five 'M's<note>original: *ma-kâra*; referring to the five ritual ingredients beginning with the letter 'M'</note></term> to be found in the <term>Sacred Scriptures of Tantra<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term>. Do these acts become blameable only if made a part of <term>Worship<note>original: *Upâsanâ*</note></term>?

All know that <term>Clarified Butter<note>original: *Ghee*</note></term> which nourishes and promotes longevity causes serious stomach-disease and even death if taken in too large quantities, whilst snake-poison, which kills, will yet cure and lengthen the life of a dying delirious man, if it be purified and given under suitable condition with a cold bath, a diet of whey, and so forth. Similarly the <term>Great Physician<note>original: *Vaidyanâtha*; an epithet of Lord Shiva</note></term> Himself has prescribed the <term>Sacred Chant<note>original: *Mantra*</note></term> of the <term>Primordial Power<note>original: *Âdyâshakti*</note></term> possessed of all <term>Powers<note>original: *Shaktis*</note></term>, and the invigorating <term>Five Ritual Ingredients<note>original: *Pancha-ma-kâra*</note></term> as <term>Spiritual Practice<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> suitable for the cure of the <term>Malady of Existence<note>original: *Bhavaroga*</note></term> of the sinful <term>Living Souls<note>original: *Jîvas*</note></term> of this dark <term>Kali Age<note>The current era in Hindu cosmology, characterized by spiritual decline</note></term>, and as a means whereby they may attain the supreme state full of eternal bliss, imperishable and immortal. All classes of physicians prescribe the use of wine, fish and flesh in measured quantities for the acquisition of strength by patients who are weak and have a low vitality. On that account the medical science does not deserve to be hated. Similarly the <term>Sacred Scriptures of Tantra<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term> do not deserve to be blamed for prescribing the <term>Five Ritual Ingredients<note>original: *Pancha-ma-kâra*</note></term> for the Liberation of <term>Living Souls<note>original: *Jîvas*</note></term> suffering from the disease of worldly existence. Shiva has nowhere said that <term>Practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> of <term>Divine Power<note>original: *Shakti*</note></term> should always drink wine, always slaughter animals and eat their flesh and always enjoy women, and that thus they will attain Liberation. On the contrary He has counselled various means for checking excesses in these matters, and He has in particular controlled license by making these acts part of the worship of the <term>Supreme Lord<note>original: *Îshvara*</note></term>. It is the degraded conduct of a number of great <term>Bound Souls<note>original: *Pashus*; literally 'animals', referring to those who are uninitiated or lack spiritual self-control</note></term> who pretend to be <term>Spiritual Practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> which is the cause of the public dislike for, and hatred of, the <term>Sacred Scriptures of Tantra<note>original: *Tantra Shâstra*</note></term>. In the <term>Great Liberation Tantra<note>original: *Mahânirvâna Tantra*</note></term> <term>The Eternal Shiva<note>original: *Shrî Sadâshiva*</note></term> says 'Wine is the <term>Savior<note>original: *Târâ*</note></term> in <term>Liquid Form<note>original: *Dravamayî*</note></term>. It saves <term>Living Souls<note>original: *Jîvas*</note></term> destroying dangers and disease, and grants both Enjoyment and Liberation. But wine, if drunk in contravention of <term>Ritual Rule<note>original: *Vidhi*</note></term>, destroys the intelligence, reputation, wealth and life of men.'

<summary>This page defends the use of the 'Five Ms' (meat, wine, etc.) in Tantric ritual by comparing them to medicine; used correctly under ritual law, they cure the soul, but used recklessly by the uninitiated, they lead to ruin.</summary>
<keywords>Tantra Shastra, Panchamakara, Shiva, Bhavaroga, Jivas, Kali Yuga, spiritual practice, ritual rules, Mahanirvana Tantra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Tantric ritual practices, specifically addressing the misconceptions surrounding the "Five Ms" (Pancha-ma-kâra). It builds on the previous page's metaphor of poison acting as medicine by establishing the strict ethical and spiritual boundaries required for such rituals.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

->INTRODUCTION<-

Even a <term>member of the Kula path<note>original: *Kaula*; an initiate in a specific Tantric tradition</note></term> who has received <term>ritual consecration<note>original: *Abhisheka*</note></term> a hundred times is to be considered an <term>uninitiated person<note>original: *Pashu*; literally "beast," referring to one still bound by worldly ignorance</note></term> and outside the boundaries of <term>the spiritual law of the Kula<note>original: *Kuladharma*</note></term> if he is addicted to excessive drinking. In the *Kularnava Tantra*, Shiva says, "O My Beloved, he who kills animals for his own satisfaction, <term>violating scriptural law<note>original: *Avidhânena*</note></term>, will live in a terrible hell for as many days as there are hairs on the animal’s body." These words from Shiva clearly show that He has nowhere authorized the unrestricted use of the <term>Five Ritual Elements<note>original: *Pancha-ma-kâra*</note></term> by the general public. 

He has prescribed the <term>Heroic Path<note>original: *Vîrâchâra*</note></term> or the <term>Way of the Kula<note>original: *Kulâchâra*</note></term> only for <term>spiritual practitioners<note>original: *Sâdhakas*</note></term> on the <term>path of renunciation<note>original: *Nivritti*</note></term> who desire ultimate liberation. These practitioners, being <term>free from duality<note>original: *Nirvikalpa*</note></term>, wish to witness the aspect of the <term>Great Goddess<note>original: *Mahâdevî*</note></term> that consists of <term>Existence, Consciousness, and Bliss<note>original: *Sachchidânanda*</note></term>; Shiva has prescribed the Five Ritual Elements to enable them to realize this state of <term>Bliss<note>original: *Ânanda*</note></term>. 

Just as a person who does not know sweetness is given sugar or honey to eat, the practitioner is allowed to taste the fleeting, <term>sensory<note>original: *Vishaya*</note></term> bliss of the Five Ritual Elements. In doing so, and by temporarily subduing his <term>six internal enemies<note>The six passions: lust, anger, greed, delusion, pride, and envy</note></term>, he may catch a glimpse of the <term>Eternal Bliss of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmânanda*</note></term>. This brief experience of eternal bliss makes the liberation-seeking practitioner eager and hardworking to achieve it fully. 

However, after attaining this <term>natural<note>original: *Sahaja*</note></term> bliss of the Absolute, he no longer craves the Five Ritual Elements and gradually devotes himself to the <term>Divine Path<note>original: *Divyâchâra*</note></term>. If a practitioner consumes wine in a limited way after it has been purified, the outward movement of his senses is weakened, and his mind or inner sense is stilled, making him ready for <term>subtle meditation<note>original: *Sûkshma Dhyâna*</note></term>. For this reason, wine is referred to as the <term>Cause<note>original: *Kârana*</note></term>. 

In the *Kularnava Tantra*, Shiva says: "Bliss is the very <term>nature<note>original: *Rûpa*</note></term> of the Absolute, and it exists within the body. Wine is its revealer, and for this reason, it is consumed by <term>yogis</term>. Wine and meat are consumed with the <term>Knowledge of the Absolute<note>original: *Brahmajnâna*</note></term> for the satisfaction of all <term>Gods<note>original: *Devas*</note></term>; whoever partakes of them for personal pleasure is a sinner." 

That is to say, practitioners perform their <term>spiritual discipline<note>original: *Sâdhanâ*</note></term> with the Five Ritual Elements for the satisfaction of the <term>Deities<note>original: *Devatâs*</note></term> they worship and the growth of divine knowledge in their hearts. But whoever consumes them for their own enjoyment is condemned to a terrible hell as a...

<summary>This page argues that the ritual use of wine and meat in Tantra is strictly a controlled spiritual exercise for advanced practitioners to glimpse divine bliss, rather than an excuse for indulgence. It cites the Kularnava Tantra to warn that those who use these elements for personal pleasure are excluded from the spiritual path and face karmic punishment.</summary>
