<meta>This page continues the commentary on the ritual requirements for the practitioner, specifically focusing on the transition between daytime asceticism and nighttime esoteric practices. It concludes the explanation of Verse 20 and introduces Verse 21, which highlights the benefits of reciting the hymn.</meta>

# Hymn to the Camphor-clad One

<page-num>28</page-num>
**With Commentary**

Being devoted in solitude, he chants one hundred thousand [times]. And again at night, being naked—meaning without clothes—and distinguished by the pleasure of intimate union <gloss>1</gloss> and the joy of sexual union, he should chant the mantra <term>manu: a sacred formula or mantra</term> one hundred thousand times. He would then become equal to the Destroyer of Desire <note>original: "Smarahara," an epithet of Shiva</note> upon this earth. If the reading is "perfectly" <note>original: "samyak"</note>, it means chanting after having performed the "Bridge," the "Great Bridge," and the "Kulluka" rituals <note>these are preparatory mantras used to "unlock" the primary mantra</note> according to the prescribed rules. || 20 ||

## Interpretation of the Essential Form || 20 ||

The practitioner <term>sādhaka: a spiritual aspirant</term> who has conquered his senses and eats only pure ritual offerings <term>haviṣyāśī: one who consumes only "havis," food fit for a sacrifice</term>, being solely devoted to the meditation on Your lotus feet during the day through the "Following Path" <term>paścācāra: the conventional or orthodox path of ritual conduct</term>, chants Your mantra one hundred thousand times. Again, at night, through the "Clan Path" <term>kulācāra: the esoteric Tantric path</term>, being naked—which here means free from the coverings of illusion <note>original: "maya"</note> and free from mental disturbances—he is distinguished by the pleasure of intimate union. "The pleasure of intimate union" <gloss>2</gloss> means ritual union; the meaning is that he is devoted to the bliss arising from the union of the Supreme Power <note>Shakti</note> and the individual Self. If he chants Your mantra one hundred thousand times, he becomes equal to the Destroyer of Desire, similar to Shiva, a "liberated soul while living" <term>jīvanmukta: one who has attained spiritual liberation while still in the physical body</term>. || 20 ||

->O Mother, this hymn of Yours, which contains the extraction of Your mantras,<-
->Is called the "Essential Form" and contains the ritual for worshiping Your pair of lotus feet.<-
->Whoever recites it at midnight or during the time of worship,<-
->Even their casual chatter flows with the nectar-juice of poetic inspiration. || 21 ||<-

## Commentary

O Mother, whoever that person is. "The extraction of Your mantras" <note>original: "manusamuddhāragajanuh"</note>: that which is the source or origin for the perfect extraction of Your mantras. "Contains the ritual for worshiping Your pair of lotus feet": including the rules for the worship of Your illustrious lotus feet. "Called the Essential Form": of Your essential nature, of the form of one's own Self; of that meditation which consists of the gross, subtle, causal, and the "fourth" state <term>turiya: the state of pure consciousness</term>, through the distinctions between the "with attributes" <term>saguṇa</term> and "without attributes" <term>nirguṇa</term>...

---

1. "Nidhuvana" can refer to trembling or to sexual union (neuter gender). — *Medini Lexicon*
2. He who is filled with the bliss of the union of the Supreme Power and the Self; that is true ritual union; others are merely indulging in women. — *Kularnava Tantra*
3. The realization "I am He" <note>So'ham</note>, which is the oneness of the individual soul <term>jiva</term> and the Absolute <term>Brahman</term>, is known by the knowers of the Absolute as "meditation without attributes." — *Shivarchana-chandrika*

<summary>This page concludes the commentary on Verse 20, explaining the dual discipline of orthodox daytime practice and esoteric nighttime ritual union to achieve Shiva-like liberation. It then introduces Verse 21, which claims that reciting this hymn grants even an ordinary person the gift of divine poetic inspiration.</summary>
<keywords>Mantra, Kaulachara, Pashvachara, Shiva, Shakti, Jivanmukta, Nidhuvana, Saguna, Nirguna, poetic inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page concludes the commentary on Verse 21 and provides the full commentary for the final verse, Verse 22, of the Karpuradi Stotram. It details the material and spiritual rewards for the practitioner and provides the colophon for the entire work.</meta>

# Hymn to the Camphor-clad One

<page-num>30</page-num>

[This verse] refers to the description or naming [of the Goddess]. He who recites <gloss>1</gloss>—that is, speaks aloud—this hymn at midnight, the middle of the night, or during the time of worship <term>pūjā</term>, for him even idle chatter <term>pralāpa: nonsensical or meaningless speech</term> becomes like the essence of nectar <gloss>2</gloss>—meaning he is filled with the taste of the nectar of poetic inspiration. His speech flows forth in the form of both prose and verse. || 21 ||

->**A multitude of deer-eyed women follow him, trembling with love;**<-
->**even a king, a representative of the God of Wealth, becomes subject to him.**<-
->**His enemy regards him as a prison through a mere playful act;**<-
->**living as one liberated while yet alive, that devotee prevails through every birth. || 22 ||**<-

### Commentary and Interpretation of the Essential Form || 22 ||

"A multitude of deer-eyed women" <note>original: "kuraṅgākṣī-vṛndaṃ"</note> refers to a group of beautiful women with eyes as soft as a doe's. They follow and go after the practitioner <term>sādhaka: a spiritual aspirant</term>, who is "trembling with love"—unsteady with deep affection. Even a "lord of the earth" <note>original: "kṣauṇīpatiḥ"</note>, a king, becomes subject to him and comes under his control; such a person becomes a "representative of Kubera" <note>Kubera is the Vedic god of wealth and treasures</note>, meaning he becomes as wealthy as the god of wealth himself. The enemy of this reciter of the hymn regards him as "a prison"—as terrifying as a place of dark confinement. Through "a mere playful act" <note>original: "kelikalayā"</note>, meaning with ease and without effort, that devotee <note>original: "bhakta"</note>—being devoted to You in birth after birth <note>original: "pratijanuḥ"</note>—becomes "liberated while alive" <term>jīvanmukta: one who has attained spiritual liberation while still inhabiting a physical body</term> for a long time, or "liberated without a body" <note>original: "videhamukta"</note>. 

Alternatively, that devotee, through a "playful act" or through joyful sport, continues to live for a long duration, maintaining his life-breaths. Having attained liberation at the end of the body's life, he becomes one whose birth has ceased—meaning he is free from the cycle of rebirth. The sense is that he attains "extinction in the Absolute" <gloss>3</gloss> <note>original: "brahma-nirvāṇam"</note>. || 22 ||

---

1. A hymn recited only in the mind, or a mantra <term>manu</term> chanted only with the voice [without the other]: both are fruitless, O Goddess, like water held in a broken pot. — *From the Vishveshvara text*
2. By reading this even once, all obstacles are completely agitated and destroyed everywhere, like moths in a blazing fire. His speech, consisting of prose and verse, flows like the current of the Ganges. — *From the Kalikula-sarvasva text*
3. This hymn was spoken by Mahakala-Rudra <note>Shiva in his aspect as Great Time</note>; the devotee who always approaches it with a spirit of devotion shall face no sorrow, no disease, and no death. In the end, there is the attainment of "Absolute Liberation" <term>kaivalya: the final state of solitude and freedom</term> and the accomplishment of all goals. — *From the Mahakala Samhita*

->**Thus ends the hymn describing the Essential Form of the auspicious Dakshina Kalika,**<-
->**composed by the venerable Mahakala.**<-
->**The "Giver of Stainless Joy" commentary and the Interpretation of the Essential Form are now complete.**<-

<summary>This concluding page describes the supernatural rewards for reciting the hymn, including poetic eloquence, influence over royalty, and wealth. It finishes with the spiritual promise of liberation from rebirth (Jivanmukta) and cites traditional sources for the hymn's authority.</summary>
<keywords>Karpuradi Stotram, Dakshina Kalika, Jivanmukta, Kubera, Mahakala, liberation, Kaivalya, Tantric commentary, Sanskrit poetry</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Karpūrādi Stotram" (Hymn beginning with the word Camphor) with a series of auspicious concluding verses and a colophon. It transitions from the final benefits of the hymn to a celebratory praise of the Goddess in her various forms and provides the lineage and date of the commentator, Vimalananda Swamin.</meta>

# With Commentary

<page-num>31</page-num>

->**She who is Kali, the beloved of Time, who removes all impurities and is praised as the destroyer of Time;**<-
->**She who is Tara, the form of the guiding star, worshipped by the lotus-born Brahma, and the supreme Wisdom of the Absolute;**<-
->**She who is Shri Vidya, the path to liberation, bowed to by Vishnu and Shiva for the welfare of practitioners;**<-
->**May that Goddess, who takes all forms, the Mother, bring forth auspiciousness for those who sing her praises.**<-

->**By this very king of hymns, described by Mahakala <note>Mahakala is the personification of 'Great Time' or Eternity, and the consort of Kali</note>,**<-
->**the essential nature of Kalika is revealed, providing bliss to her devotees.**<-
->**This hymn, which begins with the word "Camphor" <note>original: "Karpūrādyam"</note> and is infused with spiritual fragrance,**<-
->**is spoken here through a different explanation, adorned with a simple meaning.**<-
->**In the Shaka year measured by Sages (7), Eyes (2), Mountains (7), and the Moon (1) <note>A traditional Sanskrit numerical code (chronogram) representing the year 1727 of the Shaka era, which corresponds to approximately 1805 CE</note>, this was created by me,**<-
->**for the awakening of practitioners, by Vimalananda Swamin.**<-
->**This commentary, explaining her essential form, bestows the joy of Vimalananda;**<-
->**may it ever reside upon the throats <note>meaning: "in the speech or memory"</note> of the devoted who read it.**<-

->**In the town of Kotalipara <note>A historical center of learning in modern-day Bangladesh</note>, in the lineage of Shunaka, was Harihara, the leader of the assembly and the greatest among the great.**<-
->**Born of that family was the great soul Kaliprasada; his son was Raghumani, a man of righteous deeds.**<-
->**His son is Vimalananda Swamin, a follower of his father’s footsteps,**<-
->**also known by the name Satish Chandra Raya, adorned with the title of Chaudhuri.**<-
->**This commentary on the "Camphor" hymn was composed by him for the delight of the virtuous.**<-
->**May it exist for the pleasure of the Goddess Kalika, and may it bring success.**<-

->**|| Om Tat Sat Om ||**<-

<summary>This final page offers a concluding prayer to the Goddess in her forms as Kali, Tara, and Shri Vidya, and provides the colophon detailing the commentator Vimalananda Swamin’s lineage and the date of completion (1805 CE).</summary>

# The Auspicious Hymn to the Camphor-Clad One.
<note>original: "Shri Shri Karpūrādi Stotram," a famous hymn to the goddess Kali where each verse begins with a letter of the word 'Karpūra' (Camphor).</note>

## CORRIGENDA.

| Page | Line | For | Read |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 5 | 12 | Reality <note>original: "tattasva" (orthographic error)</note> | Reality <note>original: "tattattvaḥ" (correct spelling)</note> |

<term>Karpūrādi Stotram</term> <note>A 22-verse hymn to the Goddess Kali, traditionally attributed to Mahakala himself. The name comes from the first word of the first verse, 'Karpūram' (camphor).</note>
<term>Sattva</term> <note>The quality of purity, light, and harmony in Indian philosophy.</term>
<term>Seed-syllable</term> <note>original: Bīja. A single-syllable sound used in meditation and ritual, believed to contain the essence of a deity.</term>
<term>Cremation ground</term> <note>original: Pretabhūmi. A central setting in Tantric practice, symbolizing the place where the ego is destroyed.</term>

<summary>This page contains a table of corrections for the Sanskrit text and footnotes of the Karpūrādi Stotram, fixing numerous spelling errors, grammatical cases, and significant word mistranslations like 'limb' to 'wonderful.'</summary>
<keywords>errata, Sanskrit corrections, Karpuradi Stotram, Kali, orthography, Tantra</keywords>
